# Daily Summary: 2025-06-20

### Using BDF schemes in the temporal integration of POD-ROM methods
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14543v1)
- **Authors**: Bosco García-Archilla, Alicia García-Mascaraque, Julia Novo
- **Abstract**: In this paper we consider the numerical approximation of a semilinear reaction-diffusion model problem (PDEs) by means of reduced order methods (ROMs) based on proper orthogonal decomposition (POD). We focus on the time integration of the fully discrete reduced order model. Most of the analysis in the literature has been carried out for the implicit Euler method as time integrator. We integrate in time the reduced order model with the BDF-q time stepping ($1\le q\le 5$) and prove optimal rate of convergence of order $q$ in time. Our set of snapshots is obtained from finite element approximations to the original model problem computed at different times. These finite element approximations can be obtained with any time integrator. The POD method is based on first order difference quotients of the snapshots. The reason for doing this is twofold. On the one hand, the use of difference quotients allow us to provide pointwise-in-time error bounds. On the other, the use of difference quotients is essential to get the expected rate $q$ in time since we apply that the BDF-q time stepping, $1\le q\le 5$, can be written as a linear combination of first order difference quotients.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper focuses on improving the temporal integration of Proper Orthogonal Decomposition-Reduced Order Models (POD-ROMs) for semilinear reaction-diffusion equations.  It extends existing analysis, which primarily uses the implicit Euler method, to higher-order Backward Differentiation Formula (BDF) schemes (BDF-q, where 1 ≤ q ≤ 5). The key contributions are: (1) proving optimal convergence rates of order *q* in time for BDF-q schemes when applied to the reduced order model, (2) leveraging first-order difference quotients of snapshots within the POD method to obtain pointwise-in-time error estimates, and (3) demonstrating that BDF-q schemes can be expressed as linear combinations of these first-order difference quotients, facilitating the error analysis. The snapshots are generated using finite element approximations of the original PDE. The error analysis considers H¹-projections, arguing for their superiority over L²-projections in terms of eigenvalue tail estimates.  The theoretical results are validated by numerical experiments on the Brusselator system.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The extension of error analysis from implicit Euler (or at most second-order methods) to higher-order BDF schemes is a significant contribution. While the use of POD-ROM with difference quotients is not entirely new (it was employed in previous work by the authors for BDF2), generalizing this to BDF-q (1 ≤ q ≤ 5) and providing a rigorous error analysis is novel. The decomposition of BDF-q schemes into linear combinations of difference quotients is a clever technique that simplifies the analysis and is a non-trivial insight.  The application to semilinear reaction-diffusion problems adds to the practical relevance. However, similar results may have been obtained in other contexts and adapted to other numerical methods or schemes.

*   **Significance:** POD-ROMs are widely used to reduce the computational cost of simulating PDEs. Improving the temporal integration accuracy with higher-order methods is crucial for practical applications, allowing for larger time steps and, potentially, faster simulations while maintaining desired accuracy.  The rigorous error analysis provides a theoretical foundation for using BDF-q schemes in this context, giving confidence in their performance. The pointwise-in-time error estimates are also valuable as they provide more detailed information about the error behavior than global error bounds. The extension of this approach to a wider range of model problems or other kinds of complex equations is where the challenge lies.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Rigorous mathematical analysis. The paper provides detailed proofs of convergence rates.
    *   Practical relevance. The results are applicable to a widely used class of problems (semilinear reaction-diffusion equations).
    *   Numerical validation. The theoretical results are supported by numerical experiments.
    *   The use of the G-stability of BDF schemes helps to obtain rather standard estimates.
    *   The insight of using BDF-q schemes as linear combinations of first-order difference quotients.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited scope of BDF orders.  While extending to BDF-q for q ≤ 5 is valuable, the restriction is a limitation.  The authors acknowledge that BDF-6 requires separate and more complex analysis.
    *   The analysis relies on assumptions about the smoothness of the solution of the original PDE.
    *   The numerical experiments focus on a single model problem (Brusselator). Testing on a wider range of problems would strengthen the validation.
    *   The need for stability condition (23) on the time step At. Even though it is a theoretical condition, and it holds in practice, it can be a limit on the time step for higher orders of accuracy if the eigenvalues are not adequately spaced.
    *   While H1 projections are used, which allow for better bounds, in practice, L2 projections are used in general. The practical value is increased with the use of H1.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by encouraging the use of higher-order BDF schemes in POD-ROM simulations, with the theoretical guarantees to back them up. It also provides a framework for analyzing the temporal integration error in other reduced order models. Future work could focus on extending the analysis to BDF-6, exploring adaptive time-stepping strategies within the POD-ROM framework, or applying these techniques to more complex PDEs.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a solid, technically sound, and practically relevant extension to the existing theory of POD-ROMs. The generalization to higher-order BDF schemes is a valuable contribution, and the analysis is rigorous. The limitation to BDF-q with q ≤ 5 and the reliance on certain smoothness assumptions and conditions (23) prevent it from scoring higher. Also, the analysis is somewhat incremental, building upon previous work by the authors and others. However, the novel insight of decomposing BDF-q schemes and the clear numerical validation elevate its significance. Overall, it constitutes a worthwhile contribution to the field of model order reduction.


- **Classification**: math.NA
- **Score**: 7/10

### DreamLight: Towards Harmonious and Consistent Image Relighting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14549v1)
- **Authors**: Yong Liu, Wenpeng Xiao, Qianqian Wang, Junlin Chen, Shiyin Wang, Yitong Wang, Xinglong Wu, Yansong Tang
- **Abstract**: We introduce a model named DreamLight for universal image relighting in this work, which can seamlessly composite subjects into a new background while maintaining aesthetic uniformity in terms of lighting and color tone. The background can be specified by natural images (image-based relighting) or generated from unlimited text prompts (text-based relighting). Existing studies primarily focus on image-based relighting, while with scant exploration into text-based scenarios. Some works employ intricate disentanglement pipeline designs relying on environment maps to provide relevant information, which grapples with the expensive data cost required for intrinsic decomposition and light source. Other methods take this task as an image translation problem and perform pixel-level transformation with autoencoder architecture. While these methods have achieved decent harmonization effects, they struggle to generate realistic and natural light interaction effects between the foreground and background. To alleviate these challenges, we reorganize the input data into a unified format and leverage the semantic prior provided by the pretrained diffusion model to facilitate the generation of natural results. Moreover, we propose a Position-Guided Light Adapter (PGLA) that condenses light information from different directions in the background into designed light query embeddings, and modulates the foreground with direction-biased masked attention. In addition, we present a post-processing module named Spectral Foreground Fixer (SFF) to adaptively reorganize different frequency components of subject and relighted background, which helps enhance the consistency of foreground appearance. Extensive comparisons and user study demonstrate that our DreamLight achieves remarkable relighting performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the DreamLight paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DreamLight, a unified model for universal image relighting. DreamLight can seamlessly composite a subject into a new background, maintaining aesthetic consistency in lighting and color tone. It handles both image-based relighting (using a background image) and text-based relighting (generating the background from a text prompt). The model employs a Position-Guided Light Adapter (PGLA) to condense light information from the background into light query embeddings, modulating the foreground with direction-biased attention. It also includes a Spectral Foreground Fixer (SFF) to enhance foreground appearance consistency by reorganizing frequency components. The authors present comparisons and user studies demonstrating DreamLight's relighting performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**
    *   **Unified Approach:** The main strength is the unified approach to both image-based and text-based relighting. Most previous works handle these as separate tasks. This integration is a meaningful contribution.
    *   **PGLA and SFF:** The proposed PGLA (Position-Guided Light Adapter) and SFF (Spectral Foreground Fixer) modules are novel components. PGLA's direction-biased attention aims to mimic real-world lighting interactions more realistically than simple concatenation or pixel-level transformations.  SFF directly tackles a common issue in diffusion-based methods: foreground distortion.
    *  **Data Generation Pipeline:** The data generation pipeline using a combination of bootstrapping LORA training, 3D rendering, and filtering with IC-Light is reasonably novel and addresses a critical limitation in training such models (availability of paired relighting data).

* **Significance:**

    *   **Improved Relighting Quality:** The qualitative results demonstrate noticeable improvements in lighting harmonization and foreground consistency compared to existing methods. This is the most critical claim to significance. The user study results are also vital to support that the generation is also perceived as better by the user.
    *   **Practical Applications:**  Universal image relighting has broad applicability in virtual reality, intelligent editing for film/advertising, and creative image generation. The ability to seamlessly blend subjects into diverse environments, especially with text prompt control, has a clear practical value.
    *   **Limitations and Room for Improvement:**  The paper doesn't deeply analyze failure cases or limitations of the approach. The PGLA, while conceptually sound, may still face challenges in complex lighting scenarios or with inaccurate segmentation masks. While the data generation process is good, they should have described how to make it robust and scalable to more training data.

* **Strengths:**
    * The architecture is well-motivated by issues in existing methods.
    *   The results demonstrate qualitative improvements over state-of-the-art methods.
    *   The ablation studies provide insights into the effectiveness of each component.
    *   The problem is of practical relevance.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the qualitative results look good, a deeper analysis of failure modes is missing.
    *   The computational cost and training complexity are not thoroughly discussed.
    *   Some of the comparisons (e.g., against generic inpainting methods like PowerPaint) are not direct comparisons, as they aren't explicitly designed for relighting.
    *   While the quantitative results are good, the score differences are quite small in some cases. The user studies are helpful but should ideally be more extensive.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of image relighting by proposing a unified and effective approach that addresses the limitations of previous methods. The novelty lies in the unified framework, the PGLA and SFF modules, and the data generation strategy. The experimental results are convincing, and the ablation studies help validate the design choices.

However, the paper is not without its limitations. There's a need for a more in-depth analysis of failure cases and a discussion of the computational cost. Some of the comparisons could be improved. Also, the quantitative gains are relatively small, but the qualitative results justify that.
Therefore, the paper deserves a "7".

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Empirically-Calibrated H100 Node Power Models for Reducing Uncertainty in AI Training Energy Estimation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14551v1)
- **Authors**: Alex C. Newkirk, Jared Fernandez, Jonathan Koomey, Imran Latif, Emma Strubell, Arman Shehabi, Constantine Samaras
- **Abstract**: As AI's energy demand continues to grow, it is critical to enhance the understanding of characteristics of this demand, to improve grid infrastructure planning and environmental assessment. By combining empirical measurements from Brookhaven National Laboratory during AI training on 8-GPU H100 systems with open-source benchmarking data, we develop statistical models relating computational intensity to node-level power consumption. We measure the gap between manufacturer-rated thermal design power (TDP) and actual power demand during AI training. Our analysis reveals that even computationally intensive workloads operate at only 76% of the 10.2 kW TDP rating. Our architecture-specific model, calibrated to floating-point operations, predicts energy consumption with 11.4% mean absolute percentage error, significantly outperforming TDP-based approaches (27-37% error). We identified distinct power signatures between transformer and CNN architectures, with transformers showing characteristic fluctuations that may impact grid stability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, "Empirically-Calibrated H100 Node Power Models for Reducing Uncertainty in Al Training Energy Estimation":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the increasing energy demands of AI training, focusing on the H100 GPU, a current-generation accelerator. The authors develop statistical models to estimate node-level power consumption during AI training, moving beyond reliance on manufacturer-rated Thermal Design Power (TDP). They combine empirical measurements from Brookhaven National Laboratory (BNL) with open-source benchmarking data from MLPerf.  Key findings include: actual power draw during training is consistently below TDP; the power draw of transformer models fluctuates more than that of CNNs, and the developed statistical model, calibrated to floating-point operations (FLOPs), significantly outperforms TDP-based estimation methods, achieving 11.5% mean absolute percentage error. The authors highlight the importance of accurate power measurement for infrastructure planning and grid stability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers moderate novelty. While there is existing work on estimating AI energy consumption, the emphasis on empirically calibrating node-level power models for H100 GPUs using FLOPs as a predictor, and the distinction between transformer and CNN power profiles, are valuable contributions. The combination of BNL data with MLPerf benchmarks is also noteworthy.
*   **Significance:** The significance lies in improving the accuracy of AI training energy estimations. Moving beyond TDP is crucial for realistic infrastructure planning and understanding grid impacts. The distinction between CNN and transformer architectures has important grid stability implications. However, the findings are limited to a relatively narrow set of workloads and hardware configurations.

**Strengths:**

*   **Empirical Approach:** The paper’s strength is the use of real-world measurements to calibrate the power models.
*   **Addressing TDP Limitations:**  The authors explicitly recognize and address the limitations of TDP as a power consumption proxy, which is a common pitfall in AI energy analysis.
*   **Practical Relevance:** The improved accuracy in power estimation has direct implications for data center operators and grid planners.
*   **Reproducibility:** Publicly available data through the open-source MLPerf initiative strengthens the reproducibility of results, and the authors make their data and code available as well.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The analysis is focused on a single type of GPU (H100) and a limited set of workloads, raising concerns about generalizability to other architectures and larger-scale training runs. While the model performs well in the validation set, more extensive validation on newer models and datasets would be ideal.
*   **Hardware Differences:** The node-level differences between the BNL and SMC hardware create unavoidable uncertainty. While the statistical approach attempts to mitigate this, the differences still represent a limitation.
*   **Reliance on Estimated FLOPS:**  The reliance on estimated FLOPS introduces potential inaccuracies. A more granular measure of computational intensity might further improve the model's accuracy. The study only uses the log of FLOPS in the model, which potentially loses some expressiveness.
*   **Simplified Modeling of Cooling:**  The discussion of cooling and infrastructure energy use is relatively basic. The model does not explicitly include cooling power as a function of IT power, although that is implicitly captured in node-level measurements.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper could influence how data center operators and grid planners assess AI training energy demands. The proposed methodology offers a more reliable approach than relying solely on TDP. The identification of transformer architecture fluctuations could also prompt further investigation into strategies for mitigating grid stability risks.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper provides a solid contribution by improving the accuracy of AI training energy estimation. It is grounded in empirical measurements and addresses a real-world need for better infrastructure planning. However, the narrow scope (single GPU, relatively limited workloads) and data limitations (approximated FLOPS, hardware differences between the different datasets) prevent it from achieving a higher score. While the results are significant, the findings are a valuable step in the right direction, they are not a "breakthrough" contribution, and would require broader application and refinement to have truly transformative impact. The improvements presented in the model are significant, but it is a relatively simple linear model, and the significance of the work can be further improved through future studies and investigations.

- **Classification**: cs.AR
- **Score**: 6/10

### Risk Estimation of Knee Osteoarthritis Progression via Predictive Multi-task Modelling from Efficient Diffusion Model using X-ray Images
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14560v1)
- **Authors**: David Butler, Adrian Hilton, Gustavo Carneiro
- **Abstract**: Medical imaging plays a crucial role in assessing knee osteoarthritis (OA) risk by enabling early detection and disease monitoring. Recent machine learning methods have improved risk estimation (i.e., predicting the likelihood of disease progression) and predictive modelling (i.e., the forecasting of future outcomes based on current data) using medical images, but clinical adoption remains limited due to their lack of interpretability. Existing approaches that generate future images for risk estimation are complex and impractical. Additionally, previous methods fail to localize anatomical knee landmarks, limiting interpretability. We address these gaps with a new interpretable machine learning method to estimate the risk of knee OA progression via multi-task predictive modelling that classifies future knee OA severity and predicts anatomical knee landmarks from efficiently generated high-quality future images. Such image generation is achieved by leveraging a diffusion model in a class-conditioned latent space to forecast disease progression, offering a visual representation of how particular health conditions may evolve. Applied to the Osteoarthritis Initiative dataset, our approach improves the state-of-the-art (SOTA) by 2\%, achieving an AUC of 0.71 in predicting knee OA progression while offering ~9% faster inference time.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a novel, interpretable machine learning method for estimating the risk of knee osteoarthritis (OA) progression. The approach utilizes multi-task predictive modeling, classifying future knee OA severity and predicting anatomical knee landmarks based on high-quality future images generated by an efficient diffusion model. The diffusion model operates in a class-conditioned latent space to forecast disease progression visually.  The method aims to improve interpretability, foster trust, and support informed clinical decision-making by providing visual representations of potential disease evolution and localizing anatomical landmarks. Experiments on the Osteoarthritis Initiative (OAI) dataset show state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance in risk estimation (AUC 0.71), improved interpretability, and significantly faster inference times compared to existing approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Improved Interpretability:** A key strength is the emphasis on interpretability, which is crucial for clinical adoption. By generating future images and localizing anatomical landmarks, the method provides visual explanations that are more informative than purely numerical risk scores. This addresses a significant limitation of previous approaches.
*   **Efficient Image Generation:** The use of a diffusion model in a class-conditioned latent space results in high-quality image generation while maintaining computational efficiency. The paper claims a 9x speedup in inference time compared to the previous SOTA, which is a significant practical advantage.
*   **Multi-Task Learning:**  The multi-task approach of simultaneously predicting KL grade and anatomical landmarks is clever.  It enhances classification performance and provides clinically relevant information. The landmark prediction aspect contributes significantly to the interpretability claim.
*   **State-of-the-Art Performance:** The reported 2% improvement in AUC over the previous SOTA (0.71 vs. 0.69) is a positive result.
*   **Compact model:** The claim that the model is more compact is supported by the reduced number of parameters (35M vs. 215M in the SOTA method).

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** While the approach combines several techniques, the novelty lies primarily in the specific *combination* and *optimization* of existing methods (VQ-VAE, Diffusion Models, Multi-task learning) for this particular problem. The individual components are well-established.
*   **Limited Ablation Study:** The ablation study, while present, could be more thorough. For example, it would be helpful to see the performance with only the latent space image generation *without* the class conditioning.
*   **Dataset Dependency:** The method relies heavily on class and landmark annotations, which may limit its applicability to datasets without such information. The discussion in the paper acknowledges this limitation.
*   **Modest Improvement:** While SOTA performance is achieved, the improvement in AUC is relatively modest (2%). It is important to consider whether this improvement translates to clinically meaningful benefits.
*   **Limited Generalizability:** Experiments are conducted on a single dataset. Evaluation on additional datasets could help assess the generalizability of the method.
*   **Label Noise Handling:** The paper acknowledges the issue of label noise, especially with KL class 1.  However, there is no explicit attempt to address this issue, which could further improve performance.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses a practical problem with clinical relevance (knee OA risk estimation) and proposes a method that improves both performance and interpretability.  The efficient image generation aspect is particularly valuable.  The method has the potential to impact clinical decision-making by providing visual representations of potential disease evolution.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid piece of work with several strengths. The integration of different techniques to achieve SOTA performance in knee OA risk estimation is noteworthy. The emphasis on interpretability is vital for clinical translation. However, the incremental nature of the novelty, the modest improvement over the SOTA, the label noise challenge and dataset dependence reduce the impact of the contribution. The paper's potential influence lies in the improved efficiency and interpretability, which could encourage broader adoption of machine learning in clinical practice.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### AlphaDecay:Module-wise Weight Decay for Heavy-Tailed Balancing in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14562v1)
- **Authors**: Di He, Ajay Jaiswal, Songjun Tu, Li Shen, Ganzhao Yuan, Shiwei Liu, Lu Yin
- **Abstract**: Weight decay is a standard regularization technique for training large language models (LLMs). While it is common to assign a uniform decay rate to every layer, this approach overlooks the structural diversity of LLMs and the varying spectral properties across modules. In this paper, we introduce AlphaDecay, a simple yet effective method that adaptively assigns different weight decay strengths to each module of an LLM. Our approach is guided by Heavy-Tailed Self-Regularization (HT-SR) theory, which analyzes the empirical spectral density (ESD) of weight correlation matrices to quantify "heavy-tailedness." Modules exhibiting more pronounced heavy-tailed ESDs, reflecting stronger feature learning, are assigned weaker decay, while modules with lighter-tailed spectra receive stronger decay. Our method leverages tailored weight decay assignments to balance the module-wise differences in spectral properties, leading to improved performance. Extensive pre-training tasks with various model sizes from 60M to 1B demonstrate that AlphaDecay achieves better perplexity and generalization than conventional uniform decay and other adaptive decay baselines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "AlphaDecay: Module-wise Weight Decay for Heavy-Tailed Balancing in LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AlphaDecay, a novel weight decay scheduling technique for training Large Language Models (LLMs).  Instead of applying a uniform weight decay across all layers, AlphaDecay adaptively assigns different decay strengths to different modules (e.g., attention heads, MLP layers) based on their "heavy-tailedness," as measured by the empirical spectral density (ESD) of the weight correlation matrices. The underlying principle is to balance the spectral properties of different modules, leading to improved performance. Modules exhibiting more pronounced heavy-tailed ESDs, indicating stronger feature learning, receive weaker decay, and vice versa. The authors demonstrate through experiments on models of various sizes (60M to 1B parameters) that AlphaDecay consistently outperforms uniform weight decay and other adaptive methods in terms of perplexity and generalization ability on pre-training tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in its module-wise approach to weight decay scheduling for LLMs, guided by a theoretical understanding of heavy-tailed self-regularization. While adaptive weight decay has been explored before, previous methods primarily focused on time-wise adaptation or layer-wise uniformity. The insight that different *modules* within LLMs have distinct spectral properties, requiring different regularization strengths, is a valuable contribution. Further novelty lies in the way they determine which modules to penalize, by applying the heavy-tailed self-regularization (HT-SR) theory. Prior work looked into how well trained models have strong correlation in weights. This paper uses HT-SR in a more direct way for hyperparameter configurations for the first time.

* **Significance:**  Weight decay is a crucial hyperparameter in LLM training. Improving its configuration, particularly given the computational expense of training these models, is highly significant.  The paper provides a practical and effective method for optimizing weight decay, potentially leading to better performance and faster convergence for LLMs. The fact that AlphaDecay is simple to implement (requiring only the computation of ESDs) adds to its significance.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Theoretical Foundation:** The use of HT-SR theory provides a principled basis for the proposed method. It's not simply an empirical heuristic. This is further backed by the figure that shows how AlphaDecay makes the spectral properties of the modules more uniform (better).
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The extensive experiments across multiple model sizes and weight decay settings provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of AlphaDecay. Demonstrating the improvement against other adaptive weight decay methods adds further credibility. This is critical. Otherwise the paper just becomes another "we tried a thing and it worked" paper.
    *   **Clarity and Reproducibility:**  The paper provides sufficient details about the experimental setup, including hyperparameter settings, datasets, and code availability, promoting reproducibility.
    *   **Generalizable:** It shows that this framework can be applied to Adam and AdamW. This is important. If it could only apply to one optimizer, it'd make this paper less interesting.
*   **Limitations:**
    *   **Scope of Evaluation:** While the paper demonstrates AlphaDecay's effectiveness on pre-training tasks, it would be stronger if it also showed improvements on downstream tasks or transfer learning scenarios. Showing transfer learning capabilities adds a layer of usefulness to the technique.
    *   **Specific Architectures:** The experiments are limited to LLaMa-based architectures. The generalizability of AlphaDecay to other LLM architectures (e.g., Transformers with different attention mechanisms) should be investigated.
    *   **Limited Ablation:** The paper could benefit from more thorough ablation studies to understand the contribution of different components of AlphaDecay (e.g., the specific choice of PL fitting method, the update frequency).
*   **Potential Influence:** AlphaDecay has the potential to influence how weight decay is configured for training LLMs. Its module-wise adaptation strategy, grounded in spectral analysis, may inspire further research into more sophisticated regularization techniques for deep learning models.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7** to this paper.

*   The novelty is strong, particularly in module-wise weight decay adapted from the theoretical foundations of the heavy-tailed properties in LLMs. The application of heavy-tailed self-regularization theory to weight decay is a fresh perspective.
*   The significance is moderate to high, given the importance of efficient and effective LLM training.
*   The limitations regarding the limited range of architectures and the lack of a more thorough breakdown of why AlphaDecay works hold the score back. While the presented experiments are strong, a greater diversity would be appreciated.

The paper is well-written, theoretically sound, and empirically validated. It introduces a valuable technique for improving LLM training and provides a solid foundation for future research in this area.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Single-Example Learning in a Mixture of GPDMs with Latent Geometries
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14563v1)
- **Authors**: Jesse St. Amand, Leonardo Gizzi, Martin A. Giese
- **Abstract**: We present the Gaussian process dynamical mixture model (GPDMM) and show its utility in single-example learning of human motion data. The Gaussian process dynamical model (GPDM) is a form of the Gaussian process latent variable model (GPLVM), but optimized with a hidden Markov model dynamical prior. The GPDMM combines multiple GPDMs in a probabilistic mixture-of-experts framework, utilizing embedded geometric features to allow for diverse sequences to be encoded in a single latent space, enabling the categorization and generation of each sequence class. GPDMs and our mixture model are particularly advantageous in addressing the challenges of modeling human movement in scenarios where data is limited and model interpretability is vital, such as in patient-specific medical applications like prosthesis control. We score the GPDMM on classification accuracy and generative ability in single-example learning, showcase model variations, and benchmark it against LSTMs, VAEs, and transformers.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the Gaussian Process Dynamical Mixture Model (GPDMM), a novel approach for single-example learning of human motion data. GPDMM builds upon the Gaussian Process Dynamical Model (GPDM) by incorporating a mixture-of-experts framework. This allows the model to learn from limited data (even a single example per class) by combining multiple GPDMs, each specializing in a specific motion class, within a shared latent space. Crucially, the model leverages geometric features embedded in the latent space to improve smoothness and stability of motion generation. The paper evaluates GPDMM's performance on classification accuracy and generative ability, demonstrating its superiority over LSTMs, VAEs, and transformers in the single-example learning scenario. The authors explore various model design choices and demonstrate the importance of geometric feature embedding for performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents a solid, incremental contribution to the field.  The combination of GPDMs with a mixture-of-experts framework and geometric latent space embedding is novel. While GPDMs and mixture-of-experts models are not new individually, their integration and specific application to single-example human motion learning address a significant limitation of existing techniques.  The novelty lies in the specific combination and demonstrated effectiveness within this niche problem.

**Significance:** The significance is in the applicability to scenarios where data is scarce but motion modeling is essential, such as patient-specific prosthetics control or personalized rehabilitation.  The single-example learning capability makes it practical for applications where gathering large datasets for each individual is infeasible. The interpretability of GPDMs compared to black-box neural networks is also a significant advantage in medical contexts where understanding the model's decision-making process is crucial. The performance gains compared to other methods, especially in terms of generative stability (dampening and smoothness metrics), are notable. However, the evaluation is limited to two relatively small datasets.  The impact would be greatly strengthened by demonstrating scalability and effectiveness on more complex, real-world datasets.

**Strengths:**

*   Addresses a crucial limitation of existing motion modeling techniques: the need for large datasets.
*   Integrates well-established techniques (GPDMs, mixture-of-experts) in a novel and effective way.
*   Geometric latent space embedding significantly improves performance.
*   Provides clear evaluation metrics, including metrics beyond accuracy (dampening, smoothness).
*   Offers a thorough exploration of model design choices and ablations.
*   The resulting model retains the interpretability benefits of GPDMs, advantageous for applications demanding transparency.

**Weaknesses:**

*   The evaluation datasets are limited in size and complexity. Generalizability to more complex, real-world motion capture scenarios is unclear.
*   The model is likely computationally more expensive than simpler models like LSTMs or VAEs when scaled up (although sparse approximations are used). This trade-off isn't explicitly addressed.
*   The reliance on the geometric embedding might limit the model's ability to adapt to highly irregular or non-geometric motion patterns.
*   The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion of the limitations and potential failure modes of the GPDMM, especially in comparison to other generative models with different inductive biases.
*  Limited ablation study on the type of motion feature as geometry is embedded in the latent space.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a sound technical contribution with clear potential impact in data-limited motion modeling scenarios. However, the limited scope of the evaluation and the lack of a more rigorous comparison to other specialized methods (e.g., few-shot learning algorithms tailored for time series data) restrain the significance somewhat. The reliance on the handcrafted geometric initialization might also limit its broader applicability. For these reasons, it is not a completely transformative contribution. The novelty lies more in a specific implementation and application than in groundbreaking theoretical insights.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### TGDPO: Harnessing Token-Level Reward Guidance for Enhancing Direct Preference Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14574v1)
- **Authors**: Mingkang Zhu, Xi Chen, Zhongdao Wang, Bei Yu, Hengshuang Zhao, Jiaya Jia
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in reinforcement learning from human feedback have shown that utilizing fine-grained token-level reward models can substantially enhance the performance of Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) in aligning large language models. However, it is challenging to leverage such token-level reward as guidance for Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), since DPO is formulated as a sequence-level bandit problem. To address this challenge, this work decomposes the sequence-level PPO into a sequence of token-level proximal policy optimization problems and then frames the problem of token-level PPO with token-level reward guidance, from which closed-form optimal token-level policy and the corresponding token-level reward can be derived. Using the obtained reward and Bradley-Terry model, this work establishes a framework of computable loss functions with token-level reward guidance for DPO, and proposes a practical reward guidance based on the induced DPO reward. This formulation enables different tokens to exhibit varying degrees of deviation from reference policy based on their respective rewards. Experiment results demonstrate that our method achieves substantial performance improvements over DPO, with win rate gains of up to 7.5 points on MT-Bench, 6.2 points on AlpacaEval 2, and 4.3 points on Arena-Hard. Code is available at https://github.com/dvlab-research/TGDPO.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "TGDPO: Harnessing Token-Level Reward Guidance for Enhancing Direct Preference Optimization" addresses the challenge of integrating token-level reward guidance into Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) for aligning Large Language Models (LLMs). The core idea is to decompose the sequence-level PPO (used implicitly by DPO) into a series of token-level proximal policy optimization problems. This allows the authors to derive a closed-form optimal token-level policy and a corresponding reward, which is then used with the Bradley-Terry model to create a DPO loss function incorporating token-level reward guidance. They introduce TGDPO and demonstrate improved performance on benchmarks like MT-Bench, AlpacaEval 2, and Arena-Hard compared to standard DPO and SimPO. They also show that TGDPO is more robust to variations in the token-level reward model and converges faster.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the decomposition of sequence-level PPO into token-level problems within the DPO framework.  The derivation of the closed-form token-level optimal policy and its use in modifying the DPO loss are also significant contributions. The theoretical analysis to derive the loss function while bypassing the partition function offers a novel route in reward shaping for LLM alignment.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a practically relevant problem – improving the efficiency and effectiveness of LLM alignment using token-level feedback, something previously more explored in the context of PPO with explicit reward models. DPO is increasingly popular due to its simplicity; thus, enhancing it with token-level guidance is valuable. The faster convergence and robustness to reward model quality are practically important for real-world applications. However, one of the major claims in the paper is that TGDPO is more robust to variations in token-level rewards. Although this point is supported by the experiments, more analysis could be presented on the underlying reason. The claim that TGDPO improves loss convergence should be analyzed from a theoretical point of view in addition to the experimental results.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong theoretical grounding for the approach.
    *   Clear problem formulation and well-motivated solution.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across several benchmarks.
    *   Demonstration of practical benefits (faster convergence, robustness).

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The induced DPO reward might be suboptimal compared to a directly trained token-level reward model, potentially limiting the gains. The paper could explore this.
    *   The performance improvement, while statistically significant, might not be dramatically larger than some existing DPO variants.
    *   More ablation studies could be performed. For example, the individual effects of each proposed component or design choice is not carefully studied.

*   **Impact:**  If the approach proves to be generally applicable and scalable, it could influence future research in LLM alignment, making the DPO framework more efficient and robust.  It provides a promising direction for future exploration that could be influential, especially within practical LLM development contexts.

*   **Further Research Avenues:** Future studies should explore directly training token-level reward models rather than relying on the induced rewards from DPO, and comparing with other advanced DPO variants for performance on more challenging alignment tasks. Analysis of the benefits on aspects like safety and honesty in addition to helpfulness is another direction for future work.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** The paper presents a theoretically sound and practically valuable approach to enhancing DPO with token-level reward guidance. The novelty of the token-level decomposition and closed-form solution is noteworthy, and the experimental results demonstrate tangible improvements in convergence and robustness. However, the reliance on induced rewards and the incremental improvement over existing methods prevent a higher score. While a solid contribution, the improvements demonstrated are significant, but not transformative and are unlikely to become state of the art due to certain design choices. With stronger analysis and empirical validation the paper would deserve a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### GenerationPrograms: Fine-grained Attribution with Executable Programs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14580v1)
- **Authors**: David Wan, Eran Hirsch, Elias Stengel-Eskin, Ido Dagan, Mohit Bansal
- **Abstract**: Recent large language models (LLMs) achieve impressive performance in source-conditioned text generation but often fail to correctly provide fine-grained attributions for their outputs, undermining verifiability and trust. Moreover, existing attribution methods do not explain how and why models leverage the provided source documents to generate their final responses, limiting interpretability. To overcome these challenges, we introduce a modular generation framework, GenerationPrograms, inspired by recent advancements in executable "code agent" architectures. Unlike conventional generation methods that simultaneously generate outputs and attributions or rely on post-hoc attribution, GenerationPrograms decomposes the process into two distinct stages: first, creating an executable program plan composed of modular text operations (such as paraphrasing, compression, and fusion) explicitly tailored to the query, and second, executing these operations following the program's specified instructions to produce the final response. Empirical evaluations demonstrate that GenerationPrograms significantly improves attribution quality at both the document level and sentence level across two long-form question-answering tasks and a multi-document summarization task. We further demonstrate that GenerationPrograms can effectively function as a post-hoc attribution method, outperforming traditional techniques in recovering accurate attributions. In addition, the interpretable programs generated by GenerationPrograms enable localized refinement through modular-level improvements that further enhance overall attribution quality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "GENERATIONPROGRAMS: Fine-grained Attribution with Executable Programs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GENERATIONPROGRAMS, a novel framework for improving the accuracy and interpretability of attributions in text generation tasks, particularly in source-conditioned scenarios like question answering and summarization.  Unlike traditional approaches that generate outputs and attributions simultaneously or through post-hoc methods, GENERATIONPROGRAMS decomposes the process into two distinct stages:

1.  **Program Plan Creation:** An executable program plan is generated, consisting of modular text operations like paraphrasing, compression, and fusion. This plan is specifically tailored to the given query.
2.  **Program Execution:** The operations defined in the program plan are executed sequentially, resulting in the final output.

By explicitly defining these modular steps and tracking the source inputs used at each stage, the framework enables fine-grained attribution, linking generated text segments directly to their source sentences.  The authors demonstrate that GENERATIONPROGRAMS significantly improves attribution quality (both document-level and sentence-level) across several tasks, while also providing interpretable program traces that can be used for post-hoc attribution and localized refinement.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its modular, program-based approach to attribution. While program-based text generation and modular architectures have been explored before (Saha et al., 2023), GENERATIONPROGRAMS uniquely leverages this approach for explicitly improving and explaining attributions. The idea of using executable programs to track the reasoning and source integration process is a significant advancement over black-box generation or simple citation mechanisms. The distinction between corroborative and contributive attributions is insightful and highlights the deeper level of interpretability that GENERATIONPROGRAMS offers.
*   **Significance:** The improved attribution accuracy and interpretability offered by GENERATIONPROGRAMS have several important implications:

    *   **Improved Verifiability and Trust:**  By clearly linking generated content to its sources, the framework enhances the verifiability of the output, reducing the risk of "hallucinations" and fostering greater trust in the generated content.  This is particularly important in applications where accuracy and reliability are paramount (e.g., automated research, medical information synthesis).
    *   **Enhanced Interpretability and Control:** The explicit program traces provide valuable insights into the model's reasoning process, allowing users to understand *how* and *why* the model generated a specific output. This interpretability facilitates targeted refinement and debugging, enabling users to correct inaccuracies or biases by modifying specific program steps.
    *   **Potential for Post-Hoc Analysis:** The framework's ability to function as a post-hoc attribution method opens up new possibilities for analyzing and explaining the behavior of existing text generation models, even those without built-in attribution mechanisms.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents compelling evidence that GENERATIONPROGRAMS significantly improves attribution quality across multiple datasets and tasks, outperforming baseline methods by a significant margin.
    *   **Clear and Well-Defined Framework:** The program-based approach is clearly articulated and well-designed, with a strong focus on modularity and traceability.
    *   **Practical Applications:** The paper demonstrates several practical applications of GENERATIONPROGRAMS, including post-hoc attribution and localized refinement, showcasing its versatility and potential impact.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The authors provide a thorough analysis of the framework's performance, including ablation studies, error analysis, and comparisons with alternative methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Potential Trade-off with Accuracy:** The paper notes a slight reduction in answer correctness in some cases. Although the improvements in attribution quality outweigh this trade-off, it is still a factor to consider, and future work should focus on minimizing this accuracy loss. The LM-based human-correlated evaluation somewhat mitigates this concern.
    *   **Complexity:** The program-based approach may introduce additional complexity compared to simpler generation methods. Designing effective program plans and ensuring the faithfulness of module executions requires careful engineering.
    *   **Scope Limitations:** The framework primarily focuses on text manipulation and may not be suitable for tasks requiring complex reasoning or multi-hop inference.
    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** While the modular design does allow for greater control, the method is still heavily reliant on the quality of LLMs both for program generation and module execution.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The explicit separation of plan generation and plan execution offers a paradigm shift in text generation frameworks, paving the way for improved controllability and interpretability.
    *   The fine-grained attribution mechanism may become a standard approach for verifying and validating generated content in high-stakes applications.
    *   The framework's modular design may inspire the development of new text generation tools and techniques that leverage explicit program structures.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **8** to this paper.

*Rationale:*

The paper presents a novel and well-executed framework that significantly improves attribution quality in text generation. While there are some minor weaknesses (potential accuracy trade-off, complexity), the strengths of the paper – strong empirical results, clear design, practical applications, and potential influence – outweigh these limitations. The approach of using executable programs to explicitly track and explain attributions is a significant step forward in making text generation models more transparent and trustworthy. The paper lays a strong foundation for future research in this area. While the method relies heavily on the capabilities of the underlying LLM (a common concern in many contemporary AI papers), the modularity offers a degree of control and interpretability that standard LLM pipelines lack. The limitations on complex reasoning tasks are appropriately acknowledged.
Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Busting the Paper Ballot: Voting Meets Adversarial Machine Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14582v1)
- **Authors**: Kaleel Mahmood, Caleb Manicke, Ethan Rathbun, Aayushi Verma, Sohaib Ahmad, Nicholas Stamatakis, Laurent Michel, Benjamin Fuller
- **Abstract**: We show the security risk associated with using machine learning classifiers in United States election tabulators. The central classification task in election tabulation is deciding whether a mark does or does not appear on a bubble associated to an alternative in a contest on the ballot. Barretto et al. (E-Vote-ID 2021) reported that convolutional neural networks are a viable option in this field, as they outperform simple feature-based classifiers. Our contributions to election security can be divided into four parts. To demonstrate and analyze the hypothetical vulnerability of machine learning models on election tabulators, we first introduce four new ballot datasets. Second, we train and test a variety of different models on our new datasets. These models include support vector machines, convolutional neural networks (a basic CNN, VGG and ResNet), and vision transformers (Twins and CaiT). Third, using our new datasets and trained models, we demonstrate that traditional white box attacks are ineffective in the voting domain due to gradient masking. Our analyses further reveal that gradient masking is a product of numerical instability. We use a modified difference of logits ratio loss to overcome this issue (Croce and Hein, ICML 2020). Fourth, in the physical world, we conduct attacks with the adversarial examples generated using our new methods. In traditional adversarial machine learning, a high (50% or greater) attack success rate is ideal. However, for certain elections, even a 5% attack success rate can flip the outcome of a race. We show such an impact is possible in the physical domain. We thoroughly discuss attack realism, and the challenges and practicality associated with printing and scanning ballot adversarial examples.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, "Busting the Paper Ballot: Voting Meets Adversarial Machine Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper explores the vulnerability of machine learning (ML) models used in election tabulators to adversarial attacks, particularly focusing on paper ballots. It introduces four new labeled ballot datasets and trains various ML models (SVM, CNNs, Transformers) on these datasets. The study demonstrates that standard white-box attacks are ineffective due to gradient masking caused by numerical instability in common ML frameworks. The authors modify the difference of logits ratio (DLR) loss function to overcome gradient masking and conduct both digital and physical adversarial attacks.  They show that even small attack success rates can potentially influence election outcomes in close races, highlighting the risks of using vulnerable ML models in election systems. The paper further discusses the practical challenges of printing and scanning adversarial examples, emphasizing the importance of considering such attacks in real-world election scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   *Datasets:* The creation of new, publicly available ballot datasets is a valuable contribution.  There is a dearth of such datasets, especially labeled ones, in the election security community, making this a clear strength.
    *   *Gradient Masking in Voting Context:* The observation of gradient masking *without explicit defenses* and in the *voting domain* is novel and important. Previous research focused on defenses leading to gradient masking, but the authors show that numerical instability can also cause the issue.
    *   *Modified DLR Loss:* Adapting the DLR loss for binary classification is a useful technical modification.
    *   *End-to-End Physical Attack:* The comprehensive evaluation, including printing and scanning, provides a realistic assessment of attack viability.
    * *Attack scope:* Focusing on an compromised vendor and the Over attack is a realistic threat model.

*   **Significance:**

    *   *Election Security Implications:* This work directly addresses a critical aspect of election security, namely the integrity of ballot tabulation. Demonstrating that machine learning models in tabulators are susceptible to adversarial attacks, even with small success rates, is significant.
    *   *Raising Awareness:* The paper brings to light the importance of carefully vetting ML models and attack surfaces used in election systems.
    *   *Practical Relevance:* The physical attacks demonstrate that these are not merely theoretical concerns, but can have real-world impact.
    *   *Addressing Practical Challenges:* The paper explicitly discusses the practical aspects of printing and scanning adversarial examples, enhancing the relevance of the work.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   *Comprehensive Methodology:* The paper follows a well-defined methodology, covering dataset creation, model training, adversarial attack generation, and physical implementation.
    *   *Clear Problem Definition:* The paper clearly articulates the threat model and the assumptions underlying the analysis.
    *   *Thorough Analysis:*  The paper provides a detailed analysis of the experimental results, identifying key factors that influence attack success.
    *   *Reproducibility:* Releasing the datasets and software enables the reproducibility of the study, facilitating further research in the field.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   *Limited Model Diversity:* The paper focuses on common CNN architectures and a couple of vision transformers. Exploring other potentially relevant model types or configurations could further strengthen the analysis.
    *   *Specific Threat Model:* The study focuses mainly on a specific threat model (compromised vendor). Analyzing other potential attack scenarios could broaden the scope.
    *   *Lack of Defenses:* While the paper focuses on demonstrating vulnerabilities, exploring potential defense strategies against these attacks would further enhance its significance.
    *   *COTS limitations:* Exploring offset printing capabilities and a better quality scanner may provide more realistic outcomes.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper is likely to influence the design and evaluation of election tabulation systems.
    *   It will contribute to increased scrutiny of ML models used in election infrastructure.
    *   It may prompt further research on defense mechanisms against adversarial attacks on voting systems.

*Reasoning for score assigned*:

The paper delivers several valuable contributions, including the establishment of public election security datasets, and new observations of gradient masking in a voting domain. This paper brings much needed insight to an understudied topic, and deserves a high score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### NetRoller: Interfacing General and Specialized Models for End-to-End Autonomous Driving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14589v1)
- **Authors**: Ren Xin, Hongji Liu, Xiaodong Mei, Wenru Liu, Maosheng Ye, Zhili Chen, Jun Ma
- **Abstract**: Integrating General Models (GMs) such as Large Language Models (LLMs), with Specialized Models (SMs) in autonomous driving tasks presents a promising approach to mitigating challenges in data diversity and model capacity of existing specialized driving models. However, this integration leads to problems of asynchronous systems, which arise from the distinct characteristics inherent in GMs and SMs. To tackle this challenge, we propose NetRoller, an adapter that incorporates a set of novel mechanisms to facilitate the seamless integration of GMs and specialized driving models. Specifically, our mechanisms for interfacing the asynchronous GMs and SMs are organized into three key stages. NetRoller first harvests semantically rich and computationally efficient representations from the reasoning processes of LLMs using an early stopping mechanism, which preserves critical insights on driving context while maintaining low overhead. It then applies learnable query embeddings, nonsensical embeddings, and positional layer embeddings to facilitate robust and efficient cross-modality translation. At last, it employs computationally efficient Query Shift and Feature Shift mechanisms to enhance the performance of SMs through few-epoch fine-tuning. Based on the mechanisms formalized in these three stages, NetRoller enables specialized driving models to operate at their native frequencies while maintaining situational awareness of the GM. Experiments conducted on the nuScenes dataset demonstrate that integrating GM through NetRoller significantly improves human similarity and safety in planning tasks, and it also achieves noticeable precision improvements in detection and mapping tasks for end-to-end autonomous driving. The code and models are available at https://github.com/Rex-sys-hk/NetRoller .
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces NetRoller, an adapter designed to integrate General Models (GMs), specifically Large Language Models (LLMs), with Specialized Models (SMs) in end-to-end autonomous driving systems.  The core idea is to address the challenges arising from the asynchronous nature of GMs and SMs, which stems from their differing characteristics (e.g., latency, computational requirements). NetRoller facilitates this integration via three key stages: information collection (harvesting efficient representations from LLMs), information translation (cross-modality alignment using learnable embeddings), and information distribution (fine-tuning SMs with shifted queries/features). The paper proposes novel mechanisms at each stage to optimize the flow of information and reduce computational overhead. Experiments on the nuScenes dataset demonstrate improvements in human similarity, safety in planning tasks, and precision in detection/mapping. The code and models are made available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Incremental but valuable:**  The paper does not present a revolutionary change but rather a clever combination of existing techniques with novel adjustments tailored to the specific problem of GM-SM integration in autonomous driving.
*   **Specific mechanism focus:** The novelty resides in the specific mechanisms introduced:
    *   Early stopping and layered feature extraction for efficient information collection from LLMs. The instantaneous first-token, all-layer collection mechanism is a good solution to reduce information collection latency.
    *   The use of Roller Embeddings, Nonsense Embeddings, and Positional Layer Embeddings to achieve robust and efficient modality translation is relatively novel.
    *   The Query Shift and Feature Shift mechanisms offer a tunable and relatively efficient approach to fine-tuning specialized models.

*   **Adaptation vs. Creation:** While the individual components are adapted from existing methods (e.g., QFormer), their integration and adaptation for the specific task demonstrate good engineering.

**Significance:**

*   **Practical Relevance:** Integrating GMs into autonomous driving is a significant trend. This paper addresses a crucial problem in that trend: bridging the gap between the computationally heavy, abstract reasoning of GMs and the real-time, low-level processing of SMs.
*   **Performance Gains:** The experimental results are promising, showing improvements in key metrics like safety and human-like driving behavior. The significant reduction of collision rate and improved planning similarity validates the effectiveness of NetRoller.
*   **Clear Trade-offs:** The paper clearly articulates the trade-offs involved in different design choices, such as the tension between computational efficiency and information richness.
*   **Open-Source Contribution:** Releasing the code and models enhances reproducibility and facilitates further research.
*   **Limited Generalizability:** While tested on a standard dataset (nuScenes), the system might require adjustments for different autonomous driving architectures or different GMs. More experiments across diverse driving environments would strengthen the significance of the work.

**Strengths:**

*   Well-written and clearly structured.
*   Thorough experimentation and analysis.
*   Addresses a relevant and important problem.
*   Open-source availability.

**Weaknesses:**

*   Incremental novelty, building heavily on existing techniques.
*   Generalizability concerns – tested primarily on one dataset and a specific autonomous driving setup.
*   The performance improvement heavily hinges on LLMs performance. This brings the risk of hallucination, which may cause catastrophic accidents.
*   Limited evaluation – more evaluation of computational performance is expected.
*   The lack of quantitative comparison with other methods utilizing the same GM (LLaMA 2) is a significant deficiency.
*   The paper mentions the computational overhead incurred by each mechanism, the latency needs further minimization.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of autonomous driving by offering a practical solution for integrating GMs and SMs.  It introduces several novel mechanisms that lead to demonstrable improvements in performance. However, the incremental nature of the novelty, the reliance on specific models and datasets, and the presence of existing related work that is not thoroughly addressed limits its overall significance. The contribution is solid, but not transformative. More thorough comparison with other methods and testing on diverse datasets would increase the confidence in NetRoller’s general applicability.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 6/10

### Align Your Flow: Scaling Continuous-Time Flow Map Distillation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14603v1)
- **Authors**: Amirmojtaba Sabour, Sanja Fidler, Karsten Kreis
- **Abstract**: Diffusion- and flow-based models have emerged as state-of-the-art generative modeling approaches, but they require many sampling steps. Consistency models can distill these models into efficient one-step generators; however, unlike flow- and diffusion-based methods, their performance inevitably degrades when increasing the number of steps, which we show both analytically and empirically. Flow maps generalize these approaches by connecting any two noise levels in a single step and remain effective across all step counts. In this paper, we introduce two new continuous-time objectives for training flow maps, along with additional novel training techniques, generalizing existing consistency and flow matching objectives. We further demonstrate that autoguidance can improve performance, using a low-quality model for guidance during distillation, and an additional boost can be achieved by adversarial finetuning, with minimal loss in sample diversity. We extensively validate our flow map models, called Align Your Flow, on challenging image generation benchmarks and achieve state-of-the-art few-step generation performance on both ImageNet 64x64 and 512x512, using small and efficient neural networks. Finally, we show text-to-image flow map models that outperform all existing non-adversarially trained few-step samplers in text-conditioned synthesis.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Align Your Flow: Scaling Continuous-Time Flow Map Distillation" presents a novel distillation method for generative models, focusing on flow maps. The core idea is to train flow map models that connect any two noise levels in a single step, allowing for efficient sampling with varying step counts. The authors introduce two new continuous-time training objectives for flow maps, generalizing existing consistency and flow matching losses. They also incorporate autoguidance during distillation to improve performance and use adversarial fine-tuning to enhance image sharpness while preserving diversity.  The method, named Align Your Flow (AYF), achieves state-of-the-art few-step generation performance on ImageNet and demonstrates high-resolution text-to-image generation surpassing existing non-adversarial methods. A key contribution is analytically proving that standard consistency models inherently suffer from error accumulation with multi-step sampling, a limitation that flow maps overcome.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper exhibits significant novelty. The analytical proof of the limitations of consistency models in multi-step sampling is a valuable theoretical contribution. The introduction of new continuous-time objectives for flow maps (AYF-EMD and AYF-LMD), which generalize existing losses, is also novel. The application of autoguidance in the distillation of flow maps and the specific combination with adversarial fine-tuning to balance quality and diversity represent further innovative steps.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in generative modeling: accelerating the sampling process without sacrificing quality. By distilling diffusion and flow-based models into efficient few-step generators, the proposed method has significant practical implications. The state-of-the-art results on ImageNet and the demonstration of high-resolution text-to-image generation underscore the effectiveness of the approach. Furthermore, the paper's detailed analysis of the connections between AYF and existing methods (consistency models, flow matching, etc.) provides a valuable framework for understanding and unifying different generative modeling techniques. The open-sourcing of these findings by Nvidia significantly contributes to the transparency of research in the field.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Strong theoretical foundation with the analytical proof regarding consistency models.
    *   Well-motivated approach addressing the limitations of existing methods.
    *   Effective combination of multiple techniques (new objectives, autoguidance, adversarial fine-tuning).
    *   State-of-the-art experimental results on standard benchmarks.
    *   Thorough ablation studies and comparisons to prior work.
    *   Detailed explanation of implementation details, facilitating reproducibility.
    *   Rigorous comparison with other methods, that include publicly available models.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   As noted by the authors, AYF models sacrifice some single-step performance compared to methods exclusively optimized for that setting. However, the adversarial fine-tuning stage helps to mitigate this. It would be even more compelling to see comparable or superior single-step results as well, although this may be an inherently difficult trade-off.
    *   The paper relies on existing well established models, with a LoRA-based implementation. Future studies might be able to benefit from novel architectures.
    * The improvements come at the expense of more complex architectures and pipelines.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to have a substantial impact on the field. It provides a compelling alternative to consistency models for few-step generation, offering improved stability and performance across different step counts. The combination of autoguidance and adversarial fine-tuning represents a valuable strategy for balancing quality and diversity in distilled generative models. The work could stimulate further research into flow-based generative models and distillation techniques, as well as inspire new applications in areas such as drug discovery and video generation.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Guaranteed Guess: A Language Modeling Approach for CISC-to-RISC Transpilation with Testing Guarantees
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14606v1)
- **Authors**: Ahmed Heakl, Sarim Hashmi, Chaimaa Abi, Celine Lee, Abdulrahman Mahmoud
- **Abstract**: The hardware ecosystem is rapidly evolving, with increasing interest in translating low-level programs across different instruction set architectures (ISAs) in a quick, flexible, and correct way to enhance the portability and longevity of existing code. A particularly challenging class of this transpilation problem is translating between complex- (CISC) and reduced- (RISC) hardware architectures, due to fundamental differences in instruction complexity, memory models, and execution paradigms. In this work, we introduce GG (Guaranteed Guess), an ISA-centric transpilation pipeline that combines the translation power of pre-trained large language models (LLMs) with the rigor of established software testing constructs. Our method generates candidate translations using an LLM from one ISA to another, and embeds such translations within a software-testing framework to build quantifiable confidence in the translation. We evaluate our GG approach over two diverse datasets, enforce high code coverage (>98%) across unit tests, and achieve functional/semantic correctness of 99% on HumanEval programs and 49% on BringupBench programs, respectively. Further, we compare our approach to the state-of-the-art Rosetta 2 framework on Apple Silicon, showcasing 1.73x faster runtime performance, 1.47x better energy efficiency, and 2.41x better memory usage for our transpiled code, demonstrating the effectiveness of GG for real-world CISC-to-RISC translation tasks. We will open-source our codes, data, models, and benchmarks to establish a common foundation for ISA-level code translation research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Guaranteed Guess: A Language Modeling Approach for CISC-to-RISC Transpilation with Testing Guarantees."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Guaranteed Guess" (GG), a novel assembly-to-assembly transpiler designed to translate x86 binaries (CISC) to efficient ARM or RISC-V code (RISC). GG leverages a custom-trained large language model (LLM) and integrates it with a software testing framework to ensure correctness. The key idea is to generate candidate translations using the LLM and then quantify the confidence in these translations through rigorous software testing.  The approach avoids runtime virtualization overhead, supports legacy binaries without decompilation, and incorporates hardware-aware design choices (tokenizer extensions, context-aware training) to enhance translation accuracy.  The paper demonstrates strong experimental results on HumanEval and BringupBench benchmarks, showing high functional correctness and performance improvements compared to Apple's Rosetta 2 in a real-world case study.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a genuinely novel approach by combining LLMs with software testing constructs for binary transpilation. This is a departure from traditional decompilation or emulation methods and introduces a new paradigm for ensuring the correctness of translated code. The architecture-aware fine-tuning of LLMs specifically for assembly translation is also a significant contribution.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a practical and increasingly relevant problem: bridging the software gap caused by the growing adoption of ARM-based processors.  The ability to translate legacy x86 binaries to ARM/RISC-V without source code opens up opportunities for code portability, energy efficiency, and improved performance on modern hardware. The significant improvements in runtime, energy efficiency, and memory usage compared to Rosetta 2 provide tangible evidence of the practical value of the approach.
*   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The paper employs a comprehensive evaluation methodology, including diverse benchmarks (HumanEval-C, BringupBench), thorough coverage analysis using gcov, and comparison against established baselines (Rosetta 2, state-of-the-art coding LLMs).  The detailed analysis of error types and the ablation study provide valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of the proposed approach.
*   **Open-Source Contribution:** The promise to release code, data, models, and benchmarks is a major strength, fostering further research and development in the field.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Performance under aggressive optimization:** As stated by the authors, the model's accuracy suffers significantly under aggressive compiler optimizations (-O2). This limits its practical applicability in scenarios where highly optimized code is crucial.  The paper lacks a detailed investigation into strategies to mitigate this performance degradation.
*   **Generalization to more complex binaries:** While the benchmarks are well-chosen, they may not fully represent the complexity of all real-world x86 binaries. The paper could benefit from testing on a wider range of applications, particularly those with complex control flow, threading, or system-level interactions.
*   **Limitations of Unit Test-Based Guarantees:**  The "guarantee" of correctness is fundamentally limited by the quality and coverage of the unit tests. While the paper achieves high coverage, unit tests alone cannot guarantee complete semantic equivalence.  A more formal verification approach, or even integration with symbolic execution tools, could further strengthen the correctness guarantees.
*   **Missing Compiler baselines:** The authors could have provided more comparative analysis against other binary translators and compiler tools.
*  **Computational cost:** The computational requirements of the LLM training and transpilation may limit its applicability in resource-constrained scenarios. A more thorough analysis of the computational cost and scalability of the approach would be beneficial.

**Justification of Score:**

Despite the limitations, "Guaranteed Guess" represents a significant and innovative contribution to the field of binary translation. The approach of combining LLMs with software testing is highly promising, and the experimental results demonstrate its potential for real-world impact. The paper is well-written, clearly presents its methodology, and provides a thorough analysis of its strengths and weaknesses. The open-source contribution is also a major plus.

However, the performance degradation under compiler optimizations and the limitations of unit test-based guarantees prevent the paper from achieving a higher score.
