- **Abstract**: Heuristic algorithms play a vital role in solving combinatorial optimization (CO) problems, yet traditional designs depend heavily on manual expertise and struggle to generalize across diverse instances. We introduce \textbf{HeurAgenix}, a two-stage hyper-heuristic framework powered by large language models (LLMs) that first evolves heuristics and then selects among them automatically. In the heuristic evolution phase, HeurAgenix leverages an LLM to compare seed heuristic solutions with higher-quality solutions and extract reusable evolution strategies. During problem solving, it dynamically picks the most promising heuristic for each problem state, guided by the LLM's perception ability. For flexibility, this selector can be either a state-of-the-art LLM or a fine-tuned lightweight model with lower inference cost. To mitigate the scarcity of reliable supervision caused by CO complexity, we fine-tune the lightweight heuristic selector with a dual-reward mechanism that jointly exploits singals from selection preferences and state perception, enabling robust selection under noisy annotations. Extensive experiments on canonical benchmarks show that HeurAgenix not only outperforms existing LLM-based hyper-heuristics but also matches or exceeds specialized solvers. Code is available at https://github.com/microsoft/HeurAgenix.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "HeurAgenix: Leveraging LLMs for Solving Complex Combinatorial Optimization Challenges":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HeurAgenix, a two-stage hyper-heuristic framework powered by large language models (LLMs) designed to solve complex combinatorial optimization (CO) problems. The framework operates in two phases: heuristic evolution and problem-solving. In the heuristic evolution phase, HeurAgenix uses an LLM to analyze solution trajectories of seed heuristics, compare them to higher-quality solutions, and extract reusable evolution strategies to create an diverse pool of heuristics. During the problem-solving phase, the framework dynamically selects the most promising heuristic for each problem state, guided by the LLM's perception ability or by a fine-tuned smaller model. To handle noisy data inherent in CO problems, the authors fine-tune the lightweight heuristic selector using a dual-reward mechanism that combines selection preferences and state perception. Experiments on canonical benchmarks demonstrate that HeurAgenix outperforms existing LLM-based hyper-heuristics and can match or even exceed the performance of specialized solvers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach by combining LLM-driven heuristic evolution with adaptive heuristic selection in a unified framework.  Unlike previous LLM-based hyper-heuristics that often rely on external solvers or task-specific components, HeurAgenix offers a truly end-to-end optimization paradigm. The contrastive data-driven heuristic evolution phase and the adaptive heuristic selection mechanism (utilizing LLMs with test-time scaling or fine-tuned lightweight models) contribute to the novelty of the framework. The dual-reward mechanism for robust selection under noisy annotations is also a key innovation. However, the individual components--LLM prompting, finetuning, MCTS, and hyper-heuristics, are *individually* known. The *combination* of these known techniques with certain modifications constitutes the core novelty of the paper. The work presents an engineering solution for a given problem, it does not propose a new fundamental approach to reasoning.

*   **Significance:** The work is significant because it addresses a key challenge in CO: the lack of generalizability of traditional heuristics and the limitations of current LLM-based approaches. By automating heuristic design and adaptation, HeurAgenix provides a more scalable and generalizable solution for complex CO problems. The results demonstrate that it can achieve state-of-the-art performance on several benchmark problems. The dual reward design appears effective in overcoming the noisy labels which often limits such learning systems. The code and model are well-documented, and can be a valuable resources for researchers and practitioners in CO and LLMs. The practical benefits are clear: if HeurAgenix delivers on its promise, it could reduce the need for manual expertise in designing CO solvers, making optimization tools more accessible and adaptable. The extensive experiments provide strong evidence for its effectiveness.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The framework is well-designed and integrates LLMs effectively into both the heuristic evolution and problem-solving phases.
    *   The dual-reward mechanism is a clever way to handle noisy data and improve the robustness of heuristic selection.
    *   The experiments are comprehensive and demonstrate the effectiveness of HeurAgenix across a variety of CO problems.
    *   Clear and comprehensive results and tables.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Reliance on API calls to powerful LLMs may present practical limitations related to cost, availability, and rate limits. The demonstration of Qwen-7B finetuning addresses this but still involves a pre-existing LLM.
    *   While the dual-reward design improves robustness, it might also introduce biases based on how the rewards are structured.
    *   Some of the CO benchmarks might not fully reflect the complexity and scale of real-world optimization challenges.
    * The implementation of the core methods uses well known techniques.
    * There is no comparison between the performance of the different LLMs and their runtime cost.

*   **Potential Influence:** HeurAgenix has the potential to influence future research in several ways. It could inspire new approaches to hyper-heuristic design that leverage the reasoning capabilities of LLMs. It could also lead to more efficient and robust methods for online heuristic selection in CO. Furthermore, the dual-reward mechanism could be adopted in other machine learning tasks where noisy supervision is a challenge.

*   **Rigorous rationale:** The paper provides comprehensive analysis that showcases that its new approach outperforms existing SOTA, especially in specific conditions. By conducting extensive experiments, we have demonstrated that the solution not only surpasses existing LLM-based approaches but also achieves competitive results comparable to those of specialized solvers. Further, the framework exhibits scalability and generalizability, making it well-suited for practical application in complex and intricate problems.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** While the components are individually somewhat familiar, the combination of LLM-driven heuristic evolution with adaptive selection, the specific mechanisms for noise reduction, the focus on a truly end-to-end system that requires *no manual or solver integration*, the quality of the engineering, and the strong empirical results justify a score of 7. The reliance on black-box LLMs and the incremental, rather than fundamentally novel, nature of the individual components prevent a higher score. The contribution seems more of an engineering triumph and lacks major theoretical insight.
- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Comparative Study of Task Adaptation Techniques of Large Language Models for Identifying Sustainable Development Goals
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15208v1)
- **Authors**: Andrea Cadeddu, Alessandro Chessa, Vincenzo De Leo, Gianni Fenu, Enrico Motta, Francesco Osborne, Diego Reforgiato Recupero, Angelo Salatino, Luca Secchi
- **Abstract**: In 2012, the United Nations introduced 17 Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) aimed at creating a more sustainable and improved future by 2030. However, tracking progress toward these goals is difficult because of the extensive scale and complexity of the data involved. Text classification models have become vital tools in this area, automating the analysis of vast amounts of text from a variety of sources. Additionally, large language models (LLMs) have recently proven indispensable for many natural language processing tasks, including text classification, thanks to their ability to recognize complex linguistic patterns and semantics. This study analyzes various proprietary and open-source LLMs for a single-label, multi-class text classification task focused on the SDGs. Then, it also evaluates the effectiveness of task adaptation techniques (i.e., in-context learning approaches), namely Zero-Shot and Few-Shot Learning, as well as Fine-Tuning within this domain. The results reveal that smaller models, when optimized through prompt engineering, can perform on par with larger models like OpenAI's GPT (Generative Pre-trained Transformer).
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper based on the information provided.

**Summary:**

The paper presents a comparative study of various Large Language Models (LLMs) for the task of identifying Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) in text. The authors evaluate both proprietary and open-source LLMs using different task adaptation techniques: Zero-Shot Learning, Few-Shot Learning (with different sample selection strategies), and Fine-Tuning. The research explores whether smaller, resource-efficient models can achieve comparable performance to larger models like OpenAI's GPT. The authors establish a new benchmark dataset from the OSDG Community Dataset, investigate the impact of quantization, and analyze the performance of eighteen LLMs. The findings suggest that fine-tuning is the most effective approach and that smaller, well-optimized models can rival the performance of larger ones, particularly when using semantic similarity for few-shot learning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies primarily in its *application* of LLMs to the specific domain of SDG identification and in its comparative analysis of *various* models and adaptation techniques, including some recent models (LLaMa 3, Phi-3). While using LLMs for text classification is not new, the focus on SDGs and the thorough evaluation of different few-shot learning strategies (random, semantic similarity, semantic similarity with class diversity) do add value. The creation of a focused benchmark based on the OSDG is also a positive contribution. It is somewhat incremental as it doesn't propose fundamentally new techniques but rather applies and compares existing ones.

**Significance:**

The research is significant for several reasons:

*   **Practical Application:** SDG identification is a crucial task for monitoring and achieving sustainability goals. The paper provides valuable insights into the feasibility and effectiveness of using LLMs to automate and improve this process.
*   **Resource Efficiency:** The emphasis on finding smaller, resource-efficient models is important for democratizing access to LLM technology and enabling wider adoption, particularly in resource-constrained settings. The quantization experiments are relevant in this context.
*   **Comparative Analysis:** The comprehensive comparison of different LLMs and adaptation techniques provides a useful guide for practitioners seeking to implement LLM-based SDG identification systems. The insights into which few-shot learning strategies are most effective are particularly valuable.
*   **Benchmark Dataset:** The creation of an SDG-focused benchmark dataset allows for future research and comparison.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper evaluates a significant number of models and adaptation techniques, providing a broad perspective on the landscape of LLMs for SDG classification.
*   **Focus on Resource Efficiency:** The investigation of smaller models and quantization is a strength, as it addresses the issue of LLM accessibility.
*   **Careful Experimental Design:**  The exploration of different few-shot learning strategies and the creation of a balanced dataset demonstrate attention to experimental design.
*   **Publicly Available Prompts:** The publicly available prompts enhance the reproducibility and transparency of the research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Contribution:** The paper primarily applies existing LLM techniques to a specific domain. While valuable, it doesn't present groundbreaking new methodologies.
*   **Limited Exploration of Prompt Engineering:** While the prompts are provided online, the paper doesn't detail the iterative process of prompt engineering and optimization. More discussion on this aspect would have been beneficial.
*   **Potential for Bias:** The OSDG community dataset may have inherent biases, and the manual verification of the "Other" class, while necessary, could introduce subjective interpretations.
*  **Lack of Error Analysis:** A detailed error analysis to understand the common misclassification categories, would further enhance the findings.

**Score Justification:**

Overall, the paper makes a solid contribution to the field by providing a thorough evaluation of LLMs for SDG identification. Its emphasis on resource efficiency and its comparative analysis of different adaptation techniques are valuable for practitioners. However, the somewhat incremental nature of the work and the potential for dataset bias limit its impact. A more extensive discussion on prompt engineering and potential ways to address the biases would have elevated the paper further.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ProtoReasoning: Prototypes as the Foundation for Generalizable Reasoning in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15211v1)
- **Authors**: Feng He, Zijun Chen, Xinnian Liang, Tingting Ma, Yunqi Qiu, Shuangzhi Wu, Junchi Yan
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) trained with Long Chain-of-Thought (Long CoT) reasoning have demonstrated remarkable cross-domain generalization capabilities. However, the underlying mechanisms supporting such transfer remain poorly understood. We hypothesize that cross-domain generalization arises from shared abstract reasoning prototypes -- fundamental reasoning patterns that capture the essence of problems across domains. These prototypes minimize the nuances of the representation, revealing that seemingly diverse tasks are grounded in shared reasoning structures.Based on this hypothesis, we propose ProtoReasoning, a framework that enhances the reasoning ability of LLMs by leveraging scalable and verifiable prototypical representations (Prolog for logical reasoning, PDDL for planning).ProtoReasoning features: (1) an automated prototype construction pipeline that transforms problems into corresponding prototype representations; (2) a comprehensive verification system providing reliable feedback through Prolog/PDDL interpreters; (3) the scalability to synthesize problems arbitrarily within prototype space while ensuring correctness. Extensive experiments show that ProtoReasoning achieves 4.7% improvement over baseline models on logical reasoning (Enigmata-Eval), 6.3% improvement on planning tasks, 4.0% improvement on general reasoning (MMLU) and 1.0% on mathematics (AIME24). Significantly, our ablation studies confirm that learning in prototype space also demonstrates enhanced generalization to structurally similar problems compared to training solely on natural language representations, validating our hypothesis that reasoning prototypes serve as the foundation for generalizable reasoning in large language models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ProtoReasoning, a framework designed to enhance the reasoning abilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) by leveraging abstract reasoning prototypes. The core idea is that cross-domain generalization in LLMs arises from shared abstract reasoning patterns, which the paper calls "prototypes." The framework uses these prototypes to minimize representational nuances, revealing the underlying shared reasoning structures across seemingly diverse tasks. ProtoReasoning consists of: (1) an automated pipeline for constructing prototype representations (using Prolog for logical reasoning and PDDL for planning), (2) a verification system using interpreters to ensure correctness, and (3) scalability to synthesize problems within the prototype space. Experiments demonstrate improvements in logical reasoning, planning, general reasoning, and mathematical problem-solving compared to baseline models. Ablation studies support the hypothesis that learning in prototype space enhances generalization to structurally similar problems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The paper introduces an interesting and relatively unexplored idea of using abstract reasoning prototypes to improve LLM generalization. The focus on *explicitly* modeling and training on these prototypes distinguishes it from approaches that rely on implicit knowledge learned from large datasets.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results are compelling, showing consistent improvements across multiple reasoning benchmarks.  The improvements over baselines in different reasoning tasks strengthens the claim of enhanced generalization. The ablation studies contribute to the validity of the claims
    *   **Systematic Approach:** The framework is well-defined, with clear steps for prototype construction, verification, and training. The use of Prolog and PDDL is appropriate given their formal and verifiable nature.
    *   **Clear Hypothesis and Validation:**  The paper clearly states its hypothesis about the role of reasoning prototypes and presents evidence to support it. The experimental design, especially the ablation studies, is geared towards validating this hypothesis.
    *   **Relevance:** The work addresses a crucial problem in LLMs - improving reasoning capabilities and generalization across different tasks and domains.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Theoretical Justification:** While the empirical results are strong, the theoretical understanding of *why* these prototypes work is somewhat lacking. The definition of "reasoning prototypes" could be made more rigorous and the mechanisms driving cross-domain transfer could be explored in more depth. It can be argued that this lack of theoretical rigor is partly justified by the exploratory nature of the paper, however.
    *   **Dependence on Symbolic Solvers:** The reliance on Prolog and PDDL interpreters raises questions about scalability and applicability to problems where efficient symbolic solvers are not readily available. The effectiveness of ProtoReasoning depends on the quality of the interpreter and it is unclear how the method would perform when applied to domains where such high-fidelity interpreters do not exist. The dependence on symbolic solvers may limit the generalizability to real-world scenarios where problems are often ill-defined and lack a clear formalization.
    *   **Dataset Specificity:** Although datasets are generated in a scalable manner thanks to the use of PDDL/Prolog, their diversity and the extent to which they capture "real-world" complexity of various reasoning tasks could be discussed in greater depth.
    *   **Ablation study weaknesses:** Whilst the ablation studies are appreciated, they are only carried out on the Enigmata dataset. Also, the number of samples used is low (453).

*   **Significance and Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper has the potential to influence future research on improving LLM reasoning and generalization by encouraging a more explicit focus on abstract reasoning structures.
    *   The ProtoReasoning framework could be extended to other reasoning tasks and domains by developing new prototype representations and verification systems.
    *   The ideas presented in the paper may inspire the development of more interpretable and explainable reasoning models.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel approach to enhancing LLM reasoning through abstract prototypes. The empirical results are convincing, and the framework is well-designed. However, the limited theoretical justification and reliance on symbolic solvers limit its overall impact. The score reflects the paper's substantial contribution to the field while acknowledging the need for further research to address the limitations. A score of 7.5 acknowledges the strong initial demonstration and potential of the idea, but reserves higher marks for more theoretical clarity and broader applicability. A more extensive ablation study would have been appreciated.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLM vs. SAST: A Technical Analysis on Detecting Coding Bugs of GPT4-Advanced Data Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15212v1)
- **Authors**: Madjid G. Tehrani, Eldar Sultanow, William J. Buchanan, Mahkame Houmani, Christel H. Djaha Fodja
- **Abstract**: With the rapid advancements in Natural Language Processing (NLP), large language models (LLMs) like GPT-4 have gained significant traction in diverse applications, including security vulnerability scanning. This paper investigates the efficacy of GPT-4 in identifying software vulnerabilities compared to traditional Static Application Security Testing (SAST) tools. Drawing from an array of security mistakes, our analysis underscores the potent capabilities of GPT-4 in LLM-enhanced vulnerability scanning. We unveiled that GPT-4 (Advanced Data Analysis) outperforms SAST by an accuracy of 94% in detecting 32 types of exploitable vulnerabilities. This study also addresses the potential security concerns surrounding LLMs, emphasising the imperative of security by design/default and other security best practices for AI.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the effectiveness of GPT-4 (Advanced Data Analysis) in detecting software vulnerabilities compared to traditional Static Application Security Testing (SAST) tools. The authors analyze GPT-4's performance against a set of common security mistakes and find that GPT-4 outperforms SAST tools with a reported accuracy of 94% in detecting 32 types of exploitable vulnerabilities. The paper also addresses security concerns related to using LLMs and emphasizes the importance of security by design and best practices for AI. It uses McNemar's test to statistically validate the superior performance of GPT-4.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using LLMs like GPT-4 for vulnerability detection is not entirely new, as the paper itself acknowledges related work.  However, a direct comparison in accuracy with traditional SAST tools on a defined set of vulnerabilities is valuable. The paper attempts to quantify this improvement by establishing the extent to which GPT-4 excels at detecting vulnerabilities when compared with conventional SAST approaches. The actual novelty hinges on the specific experimental setup, the vulnerabilities chosen, and the rigor of the evaluation.
*   **Significance:** If the claims hold true, demonstrating a significantly higher accuracy for GPT-4 compared to SAST tools could have practical implications for the software security landscape. It suggests the potential for integrating LLMs into security testing processes to improve vulnerability detection rates and potentially reduce the window of vulnerability. It highlights a way for cost-effective method to discover vulnerabilities, especially when used along side CI/CD pipelines. The discussion on security concerns surrounding LLMs adds to its significance, considering potential attack surfaces such as prompt injection.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper provides a direct and measurable comparison between GPT-4 and SAST tools.
    *   It focuses on well-defined classifications of software bugs using the MITRE ATT&CK framework.
    *   The use of McNemar's test provides a statistical basis for the claims.
    *   It explicitly addresses the security concerns associated with using LLMs in security applications.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The study's generalizability may be limited by the specific set of vulnerabilities selected and the specific SAST tools used (SonarQube and Cloud Defense). The choice of these tools may not be representative of the entire SAST landscape. The choice to consolidate the SAST output using an 'OR' operation is debatable.
    *   The description of the experimental setup is not detailed enough. For example, the prompts used for GPT-4 are not provided, making it difficult to reproduce or assess the fairness of the comparison. More detailed info can be found in [22].
    *   The paper lacks details about the evaluation environment, including the versions of the tools used and how the code snippets were presented to GPT-4.
    *   The analysis of the LLM's potential for generating insecure code needs to be more thoroughly addressed. The potential false positives/negatives from SAST needs to be addressed.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the direction of software security research and development by encouraging exploration and integration of LLMs in vulnerability scanning.  However, the practical adoption of such techniques will depend on addressing the limitations and security concerns outlined in the paper and others.

Overall, while the paper presents an interesting comparison and highlights the potential of LLMs in security, it suffers from some limitations in experimental details and generalizability. The results are promising, but more rigorous validation and exploration of the identified weaknesses are needed before broad conclusions can be drawn.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:**

The paper warrants a score of 6 due to the potential of LLMs in the field, a clearly stated methodology, and statistical validation. The paper is brought down because it lacks the reproducibility due to not being detailed enough on the experimental setup. The limited selection of SAST tools and limited vulnerabilities also contribute to a lower score. While significant, the findings need to be qualified in the context of the identified limitations and require further investigation to fully assess the practical implications and real-world effectiveness of LLM-enhanced vulnerability scanning.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### MinosEval: Distinguishing Factoid and Non-Factoid for Tailored Open-Ended QA Evaluation with LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15215v1)
- **Authors**: Yongqi Fan, Yating Wang, Guandong Wang, Jie Zhai, Jingping Liu, Qi Ye, Tong Ruan
- **Abstract**: Open-ended question answering (QA) is a key task for evaluating the capabilities of large language models (LLMs). Compared to closed-ended QA, it demands longer answer statements, more nuanced reasoning processes, and diverse expressions, making refined and interpretable automatic evaluation both crucial and challenging. Traditional metrics like ROUGE and BERTScore struggle to capture semantic similarities due to different patterns between model responses and reference answers. Current LLM-based evaluation approaches, such as pairwise or listwise comparisons of candidate answers, lack intuitive interpretability. While pointwise scoring of each response provides some descriptions, it fails to adapt across different question contents. Most notably, existing methods overlook the distinction between factoid and non-factoid questions. To address these challenges, we propose \textbf{MinosEval}, a novel evaluation method that first distinguishes open-ended questions and then ranks candidate answers using different evaluation strategies. For factoid questions, it applies an adaptive key-point scoring strategy, while for non-factoid questions, it uses an instance-aware listwise ranking strategy. Experiments on multiple open-ended QA datasets, including self-built ones with more candidate responses to complement community resources, show that MinosEval better aligns with human annotations and offers more interpretable results.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MinosEval: Distinguishing Factoid and Non-Factoid for Tailored Open-Ended QA Evaluation with LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MinosEval, a novel method for evaluating open-ended question answering (QA) by large language models (LLMs).  MinosEval differentiates between factoid and non-factoid questions and uses tailored evaluation strategies for each type. For factoid questions, it employs an adaptive key-point scoring strategy leveraging natural language inference (NLI) to check for entailment of key facts. For non-factoid questions, it uses an instance-aware listwise ranking approach that leverages LLMs to generate silver answer instances to improve ranking performance. The authors evaluate MinosEval on several QA datasets, including self-built ones with a larger number of candidate responses, demonstrating improved alignment with human annotations and more interpretable results compared to existing LLM-based evaluation methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *separation* of factoid and non-factoid evaluation within open-ended QA. While existing work has acknowledged the difference, few have explicitly designed evaluation pipelines *specifically tailored* to each type. The adaptive key-point scoring, while inspired by existing NLI approaches, provides a more targeted evaluation for factoid answers. The generation of "silver instances" for non-factoid ranking attempts to provide a nuanced comparison and increase evaluation robustness.

*   **Significance:** Open-ended QA evaluation is crucial for understanding the real-world capabilities of LLMs, especially as they are increasingly used for complex tasks.  By improving automated evaluation methods, this work contributes to faster and more reliable LLM development and deployment. A more interpretable evaluation method, such as MinosEval, can provide valuable insights for developers into the strengths and weaknesses of the model.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addressing a Gap:** The paper directly addresses the limitation of existing evaluation methods that treat all open-ended questions the same, neglecting the fundamental differences between factoid and non-factoid inquiries.
    *   **Improved Alignment:** Experiments demonstrate that MinosEval better aligns with human annotations, which is a primary goal for evaluation metrics.
    *   **Interpretability:** The key-point scoring and silver instance generation offer insights into the evaluation process, enhancing transparency and allowing for error analysis.
    *   **Extensive Evaluation:**  The use of multiple datasets, including self-built ones to supplement existing resources, strengthens the experimental validity.
    *   **Fully automated:** The process does not require manual intervention.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The method relies heavily on LLMs for fact detection, key-point extraction, silver instance generation, and ranking.  This makes it susceptible to the biases and limitations inherent in LLMs, potentially perpetuating errors or introducing unintended biases into the evaluation process. Although the method strives to classify factoid vs. non-factoid questions, this step could also make cascading errors.
    *   **Complexity:**  The pipeline involves multiple LLM calls and modules, which can increase computational costs and complexity compared to simpler metrics. It is also heavily dependent on the "quality" of the prompting.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The current implementation might be specific to the types of datasets and LLMs used in the experiments.  Generalizability to other domains and LLM architectures needs further exploration.
    *   **Fact detection error:** Even though the factoid/non-factoid distinction is acknowledged, there may still be fact detection errors. The authors claim the overall performance exceeds 90% when the right demonstration examples are applied in-context. The results might be highly dependent on what samples are in the few-shot demonstration.

*   **Potential Influence:** MinosEval's approach of tailoring evaluation strategies to different question types could influence the design of future evaluation benchmarks and methods for open-ended QA. The increased interpretability could encourage more detailed analysis of LLM capabilities and limitations. The released datasets could encourage other researchers to examine different evaluation methods.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7** to this paper. The paper makes a valuable contribution by explicitly addressing the need for differentiated evaluation in open-ended QA. The proposed MinosEval method demonstrates promising improvements in alignment with human judgment and offers more interpretable results. While the reliance on LLMs and the complexity of the pipeline are limitations, the overall approach is well-motivated and supported by experimental evidence. The approach could also greatly benefit future work.

The score is justified by the novelty of the approach, the clear experimental results, and the potential impact on the field of LLM evaluation. While the reliance on LLMs and the complexity of implementation limit the score, the contribution is significant and warrants a high evaluation.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 0/10

### DM-FNet: Unified multimodal medical image fusion via diffusion process-trained encoder-decoder
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15218v1)
- **Authors**: Dan He, Weisheng Li, Guofen Wang, Yuping Huang, Shiqiang Liu
- **Abstract**: Multimodal medical image fusion (MMIF) extracts the most meaningful information from multiple source images, enabling a more comprehensive and accurate diagnosis. Achieving high-quality fusion results requires a careful balance of brightness, color, contrast, and detail; this ensures that the fused images effectively display relevant anatomical structures and reflect the functional status of the tissues. However, existing MMIF methods have limited capacity to capture detailed features during conventional training and suffer from insufficient cross-modal feature interaction, leading to suboptimal fused image quality. To address these issues, this study proposes a two-stage diffusion model-based fusion network (DM-FNet) to achieve unified MMIF. In Stage I, a diffusion process trains UNet for image reconstruction. UNet captures detailed information through progressive denoising and represents multilevel data, providing a rich set of feature representations for the subsequent fusion network. In Stage II, noisy images at various steps are input into the fusion network to enhance the model's feature recognition capability. Three key fusion modules are also integrated to process medical images from different modalities adaptively. Ultimately, the robust network structure and a hybrid loss function are integrated to harmonize the fused image's brightness, color, contrast, and detail, enhancing its quality and information density. The experimental results across various medical image types demonstrate that the proposed method performs exceptionally well regarding objective evaluation metrics. The fused image preserves appropriate brightness, a comprehensive distribution of radioactive tracers, rich textures, and clear edges. The code is available at https://github.com/HeDan-11/DM-FNet.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "DM-FNet: Unified multimodal medical image fusion via diffusion process-trained encoder-decoder" proposes a two-stage diffusion model-based network (DM-FNet) for multimodal medical image fusion (MMIF). Stage I trains a UNet for image reconstruction using a diffusion process, aiming to capture detailed information through progressive denoising. Stage II inputs noisy images from various diffusion steps into a fusion network, enhancing its feature recognition. The fusion network incorporates attention-guided multimodal feature fusion (AMFF) and multiscale feature fusion (MSFF) modules to adaptively process features from different modalities and at different scales. A hybrid loss function is used to balance brightness, color, contrast, and detail in the fused images.  Experiments on MRI-CT, MRI-PET, and MRI-SPECT datasets are conducted, demonstrating the method's performance regarding objective metrics and visual quality. The authors also show generalizability by extending the method to PC-GFP and IVIF image fusion.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Diffusion-based Feature Extraction:** Using a diffusion model to pre-train a UNet for feature extraction is a relatively novel approach for MMIF. The use of the diffusion process for feature extraction helps to capture finer details that might be missed in traditional deep learning training.
*   **Unified Framework:** The paper tackles the issue of needing different parameters for different MMIF tasks (e.g., MRI-CT vs. MRI-functional). The two-stage training process and carefully designed fusion modules contribute to this unified framework.
*   **Module Design (AMFF and MSFF):** The AMFF and MSFF modules are designed with the specific characteristics of medical images in mind, aiming to integrate cross-modal and multi-scale features effectively.

However, the individual components are not entirely new. UNet architectures and attention mechanisms are widely used in image fusion. The core novelty resides in the way these components are combined and utilized within the diffusion framework.

**Significance:**

*   **Improved Fusion Quality:** The experimental results suggest that DM-FNet performs well in terms of both objective metrics and visual quality, preserving details and balancing different image aspects (brightness, color, contrast). This can lead to better diagnostics.
*   **Generalizability:** The experiments on different medical image modalities and tasks (including PC-GFP and IVIF after fine-tuning) demonstrate the potential for the approach to be generalized to other image fusion problems.
*   **Potentially More Robust Features:** The diffusion pre-training might lead to features that are more robust to noise and variations in image quality, a significant advantage in real-world medical imaging scenarios.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper includes a wide range of experiments comparing against several state-of-the-art methods.
*   **Objective and Subjective Evaluation:** Both quantitative metrics and visual inspection are used to evaluate the performance of the proposed method.
*   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies demonstrate the contribution of different components of the DM-FNet architecture.
*   **Clear Architecture Description:** The method is well-described with the architectural details provided, increasing reproducibility.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Cost:** While the diffusion model is only used in training, the overall complexity of the DM-FNet might still result in high computational costs, especially for clinical applications requiring real-time or near-real-time fusion.
*   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** Diffusion models are sensitive to hyperparameters, and the optimal parameters might vary across different datasets. The paper provides information about the hyperparameters used, but a more thorough sensitivity analysis would be helpful.
*   **Limited Theoretical Justification:** While the method performs well empirically, the theoretical justification for using diffusion models for feature extraction in MMIF could be strengthened.
*   **Fine-tuning required for IVIF:** The fact that fine-tuning is required for the IVIF task reveals that more work is needed to fully generalize across different modalities. This weakens the claim of a fully unified framework.

**Overall Impact:**

The DM-FNet framework shows promise as a new approach to MMIF. Its potential lies in the improved fusion quality and the possibility of learning more robust features. If the computational costs can be reduced and the framework made less sensitive to specific datasets, it has the potential to influence future research in the area.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel application of diffusion models for medical image fusion, leading to improved performance in terms of both objective metrics and visual quality. The unified framework and ablation studies are significant strengths. However, the computational complexity, hyperparameter sensitivity, need for fine-tuning, and limited theoretical justification prevent a higher score. The paper contributes to the field by introducing a new approach, but further research is needed to address its limitations and realize its full potential.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### video-SALMONN 2: Captioning-Enhanced Audio-Visual Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15220v1)
- **Authors**: Changli Tang, Yixuan Li, Yudong Yang, Jimin Zhuang, Guangzhi Sun, Wei Li, Zejun Ma, Chao Zhang
- **Abstract**: Videos contain a wealth of information, and generating detailed and accurate descriptions in natural language is a key aspect of video understanding. In this paper, we present video-SALMONN 2, an advanced audio-visual large language model (LLM) with low-rank adaptation (LoRA) designed for enhanced video (with paired audio) captioning through directed preference optimisation (DPO). We propose new metrics to evaluate the completeness and accuracy of video descriptions, which are optimised using DPO. To further improve training, we propose a novel multi-round DPO (MrDPO) approach, which involves periodically updating the DPO reference model, merging and re-initialising the LoRA module as a proxy for parameter updates after each training round (1,000 steps), and incorporating guidance from ground-truth video captions to stabilise the process. Experimental results show that MrDPO significantly enhances video-SALMONN 2's captioning accuracy, reducing the captioning error rates by 28\%. The final video-SALMONN 2 model, with just 7 billion parameters, surpasses leading models such as GPT-4o and Gemini-1.5-Pro in video captioning tasks, while maintaining highly competitive performance to the state-of-the-art on widely used video question-answering benchmarks among models of similar size. Codes are available at \href{https://github.com/bytedance/video-SALMONN-2}{https://github.com/bytedance/video-SALMONN-2}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the video-SALMONN 2 paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces video-SALMONN 2, an enhanced audio-visual large language model (LLM) designed for improved video captioning, particularly through the use of directed preference optimization (DPO). A key contribution is a novel multi-round DPO (MrDPO) approach, which involves periodically updating the DPO reference model, merging and re-initializing LoRA modules, and incorporating ground-truth captions to stabilize training. The authors also propose new metrics for evaluating the completeness and accuracy of video descriptions.  Experimental results suggest that MrDPO significantly enhances video-SALMONN 2's captioning accuracy, allowing it to surpass larger commercial models (GPT-4o, Gemini-1.5 Pro) in video captioning tasks while maintaining competitive performance on video question answering (QA) benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper has a reasonable degree of novelty. While audio-visual LLMs are an active research area, the specific combination of techniques presented here is relatively new. The primary novelty stems from the MrDPO method with LoRA merging and re-initialization, and the proposal for tailored metrics optimized through DPO.  The integration of audio into a visual LLM is not completely novel, but the focus on captioning accuracy and the architecture's specific design contribute to its distinctiveness. The design of metrics around atomic events demonstrates innovation in evaluation methodology.

* **Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in its potential to improve the accuracy and detail of video captions generated by LLMs.  Accurate and comprehensive video descriptions are crucial for various applications, including video search, accessibility, and automated content understanding. The experimental results demonstrate that video-SALMONN 2 outperforms existing models, suggesting a tangible improvement in video captioning quality.  The proposed MrDPO approach could be generalized to other multimodal tasks where DPO is used, offering a potential advance in RL-based fine-tuning. The released code and dataset contribute to the community's ability to build and evaluate such models.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Strong empirical results:** The paper presents compelling experimental results demonstrating the superiority of video-SALMONN 2 over competing models, including larger commercial ones. The ablation studies support the effectiveness of the MrDPO approach.
    * **Clearly defined methods:** The proposed MrDPO approach and the new evaluation metrics are well-defined and explained.
    * **Detailed architecture:** The architecture is described clearly, aiding in understanding.
    * **Availability of code and data:**  Releasing the code and a new video captioning benchmark makes the work more accessible and impactful for other researchers.
    * **Good ablations:** Ablation studies are included for multiple important aspects like gDPO versus DPO and continuous merging of LoRA parameters, adding value to the claims made.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental improvements:** While the combination of techniques is relatively novel, each individual component (DPO, LoRA, Audio alignment) is not groundbreaking in isolation. The novelty lies in the intelligent integration and adaptation of these established methods for the video captioning task.
    * **Video QA performance:** The limitations of video-SALMONN 2 for video QA compared to captioning is a bit concerning. This suggests either captioning and video QA are different tasks or the models training methodology isn't fully effective for both tasks.
    * **Lack of rigorous generalization testing:** While results are promising, the lack of experiments on a larger range of existing and diverse video datasets is a weakness.
    * **Dependence on large LLMs:** A weakness for many current approaches, the proposed method requires high-performing LLMs like GPT-4 and GPT-3.5 for evaluation and training data generation, which may limit accessibility.

* **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more accurate and detailed video captioning systems. The MrDPO approach could be adopted by other researchers working on multimodal LLMs, and the proposed evaluation metrics could become a standard for assessing video captioning quality. The released code and dataset should spur further research in this area.

**Score:** 7/10

**Rationale:** The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of audio-visual LLMs for video captioning. The MrDPO approach and tailored evaluation metrics are valuable additions. While the individual components are not revolutionary, the effective integration and demonstrated performance gains justify a positive evaluation. The paper's weaknesses are primarily the reliance on powerful closed source LLMs, the rather incremental advancement over previous approaches, and the somewhat limited scope of the generalizability testing on other video datasets.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Large Language Models for Unit Testing: A Systematic Literature Review
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15227v1)
- **Authors**: Quanjun Zhang, Chunrong Fang, Siqi Gu, Ye Shang, Zhenyu Chen, Liang Xiao
- **Abstract**: Unit testing is a fundamental practice in modern software engineering, with the aim of ensuring the correctness, maintainability, and reliability of individual software components. Very recently, with the advances in Large Language Models (LLMs), a rapidly growing body of research has leveraged LLMs to automate various unit testing tasks, demonstrating remarkable performance and significantly reducing manual effort. However, due to ongoing explorations in the LLM-based unit testing field, it is challenging for researchers to understand existing achievements, open challenges, and future opportunities. This paper presents the first systematic literature review on the application of LLMs in unit testing until March 2025. We analyze \numpaper{} relevant papers from the perspectives of both unit testing and LLMs. We first categorize existing unit testing tasks that benefit from LLMs, e.g., test generation and oracle generation. We then discuss several critical aspects of integrating LLMs into unit testing research, including model usage, adaptation strategies, and hybrid approaches. We further summarize key challenges that remain unresolved and outline promising directions to guide future research in this area. Overall, our paper provides a systematic overview of the research landscape to the unit testing community, helping researchers gain a comprehensive understanding of achievements and promote future research. Our artifacts are publicly available at the GitHub repository: https://github.com/iSEngLab/AwesomeLLM4UT.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a systematic literature review of the application of Large Language Models (LLMs) in unit testing. It analyzes 105 relevant papers published up to March 2025.  The review categorizes unit testing tasks that benefit from LLMs (e.g., test generation, oracle generation), discusses aspects of integrating LLMs into unit testing (model usage, adaptation strategies, hybrid approaches), and highlights challenges and future research directions.  The paper aims to provide a comprehensive overview of the research landscape to the unit testing community, helping researchers understand achievements and promote future research. Key aspects analyzed include publication trends, the distribution of unit testing tasks addressed, commonly used LLMs, prompt engineering techniques, and accompanying methods. The paper also identifies key challenges and potential opportunities for future research in this area.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper claims to be the *first* systematic literature review focused specifically on the application of LLMs to *unit testing*. This is a strong claim to novelty. While other surveys on LLMs in SE exist, a dedicated review targeting the *specifics* of unit testing seems genuinely new, considering the rapid advancements in this niche. Also, existing surveys for unit testing are from a pre-LLM era.
*   **Significance:**  The significance of the paper stems from several factors:

    *   **Timeliness:** The LLM-based unit testing field is rapidly evolving. A review that synthesizes existing work is valuable for navigating this complexity.
    *   **Comprehensive Scope:** The review covers a broad range of unit testing tasks and LLM integration techniques, providing a holistic view. The analysis from both a unit testing perspective *and* an LLM perspective adds depth.
    *   **Practical Value:** Identification of challenges and future opportunities can guide researchers and practitioners in addressing limitations and pursuing promising directions.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper outlines a well-defined systematic literature review methodology, including search strategy, inclusion/exclusion criteria, and quality assessment. This enhances the rigor and reliability of the review.
    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The inclusion of 105 papers suggests a reasonably thorough search and selection process.
    *   **Structured Analysis:**  The categorization of unit testing tasks and LLM utilization strategies provides a clear framework for understanding the field.
    *   **Balanced Perspective:** The dual analysis from unit testing and LLM viewpoints offers a comprehensive understanding.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Potential for Bias:** Although the methodology is well-defined, any systematic review is susceptible to bias in the selection and interpretation of studies.  The authors acknowledge this with the discussion of non-peer reviewed papers and their varying quality, suggesting that they took efforts to be objective.
    *   **Limited Depth of Technical Analysis:** While the review provides a broad overview, it could potentially benefit from a deeper dive into the technical details of individual approaches and a more critical comparative analysis of their strengths and weaknesses.
    *   **Dependence on Published Literature:** The review is inherently limited by the scope and quality of existing publications. It may not fully capture unpublished findings or ongoing work. A lack of a detailed description on how the biases of the dataset where minimised to strengthen the reliability of the analysis.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to be a valuable resource for researchers entering the field of LLM-based unit testing. It can help them quickly grasp the state-of-the-art, identify open problems, and build upon existing work. It can also inform practitioners about the potential benefits and limitations of using LLMs in their unit testing workflows.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field by providing the *first* systematic literature review of LLM-based unit testing. While it has some limitations related to potential biases and depth of technical analysis, the paper demonstrates a clear methodology, comprehensive coverage, structured analysis, and balanced perspectives. This paper will help the direction of future research within the community as well as aid new researchers to understand what has been accomplished thus far.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### Lost in Variation? Evaluating NLI Performance in Basque and Spanish Geographical Variants
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15239v1)
- **Authors**: Jaione Bengoetxea, Itziar Gonzalez-Dios, Rodrigo Agerri
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we evaluate the capacity of current language technologies to understand Basque and Spanish language varieties. We use Natural Language Inference (NLI) as a pivot task and introduce a novel, manually-curated parallel dataset in Basque and Spanish, along with their respective variants. Our empirical analysis of crosslingual and in-context learning experiments using encoder-only and decoder-based Large Language Models (LLMs) shows a performance drop when handling linguistic variation, especially in Basque. Error analysis suggests that this decline is not due to lexical overlap, but rather to the linguistic variation itself. Further ablation experiments indicate that encoder-only models particularly struggle with Western Basque, which aligns with linguistic theory that identifies peripheral dialects (e.g., Western) as more distant from the standard. All data and code are publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how well current language technologies understand geographical variations in Basque and Spanish. It introduces XNLIvar, a new, manually curated parallel dataset in Basque and Spanish, along with their respective dialectal variations. Using Natural Language Inference (NLI) as a task, the authors evaluate cross-lingual and in-context learning with encoder-only and decoder-based Large Language Models (LLMs). Their results reveal a performance drop when processing linguistic variation, especially in Basque.  Error analysis suggests that this decline isn't due to lexical overlap but rather to linguistic variations. Ablation experiments further indicate that encoder-only models struggle particularly with Western Basque, aligning with linguistic theory identifying peripheral dialects as more distant from the standard. The authors also note that Spanish benefits from a greater presence in pre-training corpora, leading to better performance despite variations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses an important and often overlooked issue in NLP: the handling of language variation. The creation of the XNLIvar dataset is a valuable contribution, providing a much-needed resource for evaluating models on non-standard language. The methodology is sound, using NLI as a proxy task to assess language understanding and employing various experimental setups (cross-lingual transfer, in-context learning) with state-of-the-art LLMs. The error analysis provides useful insights into the challenges posed by linguistic variation. The observations regarding the impact of Western Basque's divergence and the benefits of higher representation of Spanish are well-reasoned.

However, the paper has some limitations. The dataset, while valuable, is relatively small, with a limited number of speakers contributing variations. The focus is primarily on geographical variation, neglecting other types like sociolectal variation. The conclusion about LLM performance on Spanish variants being attributed to pre-training data representation is plausible but lacks strong empirical evidence beyond the observed performance. Further investigation, potentially examining the composition of pre-training corpora or using controlled experiments, would strengthen this argument. The paper also notes that speakers creating variations have linguistic and NLP backgrounds which would also affect outcomes from more "natural" language data.

Despite these limitations, the paper is a significant step forward in highlighting the importance of addressing language variation in NLP. It provides a solid foundation for future research and a valuable dataset for benchmarking models on non-standard language. It also correctly identifies the potential for language technology to reinforce existing biases if it is not sensitive to language variation.

Score: 7

**Justification:**

A score of 7 reflects the paper's solid contribution balanced against the identified limitations.  The novelty lies in the curated dataset and the empirical analysis of LLMs on Basque and Spanish dialects using NLI. The significance comes from its highlighting the need to address language variation to avoid creating potentially discriminatory language technologies.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Creation of a valuable, manually curated dataset (XNLIvar).
    *   Rigorous experimental methodology.
    *   Useful insights into the challenges of handling linguistic variation.
    *   Demonstrates the impact of data distribution (pre-training corpora).
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Relatively small dataset.
    *   Limited scope (focuses primarily on geographical variation).
    *   Claims regarding Spanish performance benefits from pre-training data need stronger evidence.
    *   Backgrounds of speakers/annotators limit "natural" data creation.

The paper's potential influence on the field is to encourage more research on language variation and the development of more robust and inclusive NLP systems. While the findings may not be groundbreaking, the work provides a valuable resource and a clear demonstration of the challenges that lie ahead, and the impact that could be had by further research.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Research on Graph-Retrieval Augmented Generation Based on Historical Text Knowledge Graphs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15241v1)
- **Authors**: Yang Fan, Zhang Qi, Xing Wenqian, Liu Chang, Liu Liu
- **Abstract**: This article addresses domain knowledge gaps in general large language models for historical text analysis in the context of computational humanities and AIGC technology. We propose the Graph RAG framework, combining chain-of-thought prompting, self-instruction generation, and process supervision to create a The First Four Histories character relationship dataset with minimal manual annotation. This dataset supports automated historical knowledge extraction, reducing labor costs. In the graph-augmented generation phase, we introduce a collaborative mechanism between knowledge graphs and retrieval-augmented generation, improving the alignment of general models with historical knowledge. Experiments show that the domain-specific model Xunzi-Qwen1.5-14B, with Simplified Chinese input and chain-of-thought prompting, achieves optimal performance in relation extraction (F1 = 0.68). The DeepSeek model integrated with GraphRAG improves F1 by 11% (0.08-0.19) on the open-domain C-CLUE relation extraction dataset, surpassing the F1 value of Xunzi-Qwen1.5-14B (0.12), effectively alleviating hallucinations phenomenon, and improving interpretability. This framework offers a low-resource solution for classical text knowledge extraction, advancing historical knowledge services and humanities research.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Research on Graph-Retrieval Augmented Generation Based on Historical Text Knowledge Graphs":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the limitations of general large language models (LLMs) when applied to historical text analysis, specifically in extracting knowledge and understanding relationships between historical figures.  The authors propose a Graph Retrieval-Augmented Generation (GraphRAG) framework to improve performance.  The framework combines chain-of-thought prompting, self-instruction generation, and process supervision to create a character relationship dataset from "The First Four Histories" with minimal manual annotation. The GraphRAG system integrates historical character knowledge graphs with retrieval-augmented generation, aiming to align general models with historical knowledge. The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of their approach through experiments, showing improved performance in relation extraction compared to general LLMs. They highlight the use of domain-specific models and the reduction of "hallucinations" in LLM outputs. The authors argue their framework offers a low-resource solution for classical text knowledge extraction, advancing historical knowledge services and humanities research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a relevant problem:**  The paper tackles a specific and important challenge in applying LLMs to specialized domains: the lack of domain-specific knowledge and the tendency to "hallucinate." Historical text analysis is a compelling application area.
    *   **Well-defined methodology:** The paper clearly outlines the GraphRAG framework and its components.  The combination of knowledge graph construction, retrieval augmentation, and various prompting strategies is logical.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experiments comparing different models and prompting strategies provides quantitative evidence for the effectiveness of their approach. The performance gains observed with the domain-specific fine-tuned model (Xunzi-Qwen1.5-14B) are significant. The improvement observed with GraphRAG over other techniques strengthens their argument.
    *   **Practical contribution:** The proposed framework provides a practical approach for extracting knowledge from historical texts, which can be valuable for researchers in the humanities. The creation of a relationship dataset from "The First Four Histories" is a useful resource.
    *   **Thorough Evaluation:** The paper evaluates the system using multiple metrics (F1, ROUGE), providing a comprehensive view of the system's performance and using both a development-focused dataset and a general-domain one (C-CLUE) for robustness.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty in Components:** While the *combination* of techniques is novel, the individual components (knowledge graphs, RAG, chain-of-thought prompting) are well-established. The paper doesn't necessarily present fundamentally new algorithms or methods within these components.
    *   **Dataset Specificity:** The evaluation focuses primarily on "The First Four Histories." While this is a valid starting point, the generalizability of the findings to other historical periods or types of texts could be questioned. The success of the domain-specific fine-tuning might be highly dependent on the characteristics of this particular corpus.  The C-CLUE test helps, but it is a different extraction problem entirely.
    *   **Computational Resource Requirements:** The paper mentions this limitation, but it's a significant one.  The need for substantial computational resources limits the accessibility and wider adoption of the proposed framework, especially for researchers without access to high-end computing infrastructure.  The discussion of future work regarding model compression techniques addresses this, but it remains a current limitation.
    *   **Evaluation Rigor for GraphRAG:** The comparison of DeepSeek with and without GraphRAG is good, but it would have been useful to also compare against other RAG variations that don't use knowledge graphs (e.g., RAG with a dense retriever over unstructured text). This would better isolate the contribution of the graph-based retrieval.
    *   **Clarity in Hyperparameter Selection:** While Table 3 lists the hyperparameters, the *justification* for choosing these values is not thoroughly discussed. Were these values the result of a hyperparameter optimization process? If so, what was the methodology?

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper contributes to the growing body of research on applying LLMs to specialized domains. It provides a practical approach for overcoming the limitations of general-purpose models in handling domain-specific knowledge.
    *   The study highlights the importance of domain-specific fine-tuning and knowledge integration for achieving high performance in tasks like historical text analysis.
    *   The paper offers a valuable resource (character relationship dataset) for researchers in the humanities.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by combining established techniques into a cohesive framework for historical text analysis. The experimental results are convincing, and the practical implications are clear. However, the limited novelty in the individual components and the specificity of the evaluation dataset, along with the computational resource requirements, hold it back from being a truly exceptional contribution.

**Score: 7**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Unlocking Post-hoc Dataset Inference with Synthetic Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15271v1)
- **Authors**: Bihe Zhao, Pratyush Maini, Franziska Boenisch, Adam Dziedzic
- **Abstract**: The remarkable capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) can be mainly attributed to their massive training datasets, which are often scraped from the internet without respecting data owners' intellectual property rights. Dataset Inference (DI) offers a potential remedy by identifying whether a suspect dataset was used in training, thereby enabling data owners to verify unauthorized use. However, existing DI methods require a private set-known to be absent from training-that closely matches the compromised dataset's distribution. Such in-distribution, held-out data is rarely available in practice, severely limiting the applicability of DI. In this work, we address this challenge by synthetically generating the required held-out set. Our approach tackles two key obstacles: (1) creating high-quality, diverse synthetic data that accurately reflects the original distribution, which we achieve via a data generator trained on a carefully designed suffix-based completion task, and (2) bridging likelihood gaps between real and synthetic data, which is realized through post-hoc calibration. Extensive experiments on diverse text datasets show that using our generated data as a held-out set enables DI to detect the original training sets with high confidence, while maintaining a low false positive rate. This result empowers copyright owners to make legitimate claims on data usage and demonstrates our method's reliability for real-world litigations. Our code is available at https://github.com/sprintml/PostHocDatasetInference.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces a method to address the challenge of lacking in-distribution held-out data for dataset inference (DI) attacks on Large Language Models (LLMs).  The core idea is to synthetically generate the held-out data using a data generator trained on a suffix-based completion task.  To bridge the gap between real and synthetic data, the authors employ a post-hoc calibration technique using a dual-classifier approach: one classifier distinguishes between real and synthetic text, and the other incorporates both text and membership signals.  The difference in performance between these classifiers is then used as the DI signal.  The method is evaluated on diverse text datasets, showing it can detect training data use with high confidence and low false positives.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The idea of using synthetic data for DI is novel and tackles a practical limitation of existing methods. The suffix-completion approach is a reasonable choice for generating relatively realistic data, and the dual-classifier calibration addresses the distribution shift between real and synthetic data, which is a crucial problem in MIA. The approach to weight constrain also seems well motivated.

**Significance:**  The significance of this work lies in its potential to make DI more applicable in real-world copyright infringement scenarios.  The lack of suitable held-out data is a major barrier to the practical use of DI, and this paper offers a viable solution. The ability for content creators to verify unauthorized use of their data in LLM training is crucial for protecting intellectual property rights. This capability also provides much needed transparency in the increasingly closed source LLM landscape.

**Strengths:**
*   **Addresses a key limitation:** Directly tackles the core challenge of requiring in-distribution held-out data for DI.
*   **Well-motivated approach:** The choice of suffix completion and dual-classifier calibration is justified.
*   **Comprehensive evaluation:** Experiments on diverse datasets and throughly ablated, further supporting the methodology's robustness.

**Weaknesses:**
*   **Complexity:** The method is relatively complex, requiring training a generator and two classifiers, which may increase the barrier to entry. The calibration technique also requires sufficient data to train reliable classifiers.
*   **Potential for adversarial generation:** While the dual-classifier calibration aims to mitigate distribution shifts, a sufficiently sophisticated adversary could potentially craft synthetic data that exploits biases in the classifiers to evade detection. However, this is the case with all MIA and DI techniques.
*   **Dependence on the generator's quality:** The effectiveness of the method ultimately depends on the quality of the generated data. If the generator fails to capture crucial aspects of the original data's distribution, the DI performance may suffer. While the suffix-completion method is a good starting point, there are more complex generation models that may further improve results.
*   **Limited evaluation on extremely large datasets:** The primary evaluation dataset involves blog posts and pile, it may be interesting to investigate on larger datasets with high-dimensionality text.

**Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of LLM security and privacy.  By providing a practical way to perform DI without requiring private held-out data, it could empower data owners to monitor and enforce their copyright. This transparency is crucial for enabling responsible AI development and building trust in LLM technology. As such, this work could lead to further research in generative data for security applications and more advanced calibration techniques.

**Justification for Score:** While the paper presents a novel and significant contribution, the inherent complexity of the method and the reliance on a well-trained data generator limit its immediate widespread adoption. Furthermore, the vulnerability to more sophisticated adversarial data generation needs to be addressed. However, given the novelty and the practical impact the method achieves in solving a fundamental bottleneck in dataset inference for LLMs, a score of 7 is justified.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Human Motion Capture from Loose and Sparse Inertial Sensors with Garment-aware Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15290v1)
- **Authors**: Andela Ilic, Jiaxi Jiang, Paul Streli, Xintong Liu, Christian Holz
- **Abstract**: Motion capture using sparse inertial sensors has shown great promise due to its portability and lack of occlusion issues compared to camera-based tracking. Existing approaches typically assume that IMU sensors are tightly attached to the human body. However, this assumption often does not hold in real-world scenarios. In this paper, we present a new task of full-body human pose estimation using sparse, loosely attached IMU sensors. To solve this task, we simulate IMU recordings from an existing garment-aware human motion dataset. We developed transformer-based diffusion models to synthesize loose IMU data and estimate human poses based on this challenging loose IMU data. In addition, we show that incorporating garment-related parameters while training the model on simulated loose data effectively maintains expressiveness and enhances the ability to capture variations introduced by looser or tighter garments. Experiments show that our proposed diffusion methods trained on simulated and synthetic data outperformed the state-of-the-art methods quantitatively and qualitatively, opening up a promising direction for future research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of full-body human pose estimation using sparse Inertial Measurement Units (IMUs) that are *loosely* attached to clothing. This is a more realistic scenario than existing methods that assume tightly strapped IMUs. The key idea is to use diffusion models, trained on a combination of simulated, synthetic, and real-world data, to estimate poses from the noisy and uncertain IMU data. The approach involves simulating IMU readings from garment-aware human motion datasets, using a secondary diffusion model to generate realistic loose-wear IMU signals, and then training a primary diffusion model conditioned on the loose IMU data (and garment parameters) to estimate poses.  Experiments demonstrate the approach outperforms state-of-the-art methods, particularly demonstrating robustness to variations in garment fit.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel task – full-body pose estimation from sparse, loosely attached IMUs.  Prior work either focused on tightly attached IMUs or only addressed upper-body pose.  The use of conditional diffusion models for this specific problem and the strategy of combining simulated, synthetic, and real-world data for training are also novel elements.  However, diffusion models are increasingly common for human motion-related tasks, so the *architecture* is not a radical departure. The idea of using a secondary diffusion model to generate loose-wear IMU data builds upon ideas from the existing LIP paper, but adds pose conditioning.  The incorporation of garment parameters is a worthwhile extension.

*   **Significance:**  The work has the potential to significantly impact human motion capture in real-world settings.  Loosely attached IMUs are far more practical and comfortable for users than tightly strapped ones. The improved robustness to garment variations expands the applicability of IMU-based motion capture. The real-time performance, while employing a computationally demanding technique, is a relevant contribution for real-world usability. The ablation studies give good insights into the effectiveness of the different components.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper tackles a practical and important problem.
    *   The use of diffusion models is well-motivated and leads to improved results.
    *   The training strategy combining different data sources is effective.
    *   The evaluation is comprehensive, using multiple datasets and metrics.
    *   The paper includes ablation studies to analyze the contribution of different components.
    *   The experiments look detailed and show that the proposed approach outperforms the state-of-the-art approaches.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on TailorNet for garment simulation is a potential limitation. The accuracy and realism of TailorNet might affect the quality of the simulated IMU data and the performance of the trained models.
    *   The paper would benefit from a more detailed discussion of the computational cost of the diffusion models and how it impacts real-time performance. How much does the sliding-window approach really help? Also, this sliding window approach is borrowed from previous works.
    *   The discussion of the limitations of the garment-aware model, particularly regarding the lack of detailed participant physique data, could be expanded. The current evaluation may not fully capture the benefits (or drawbacks) of this model in diverse real-world scenarios. The model has a strong need for a dataset.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could inspire further research on pose estimation with loosely attached sensors, the development of more realistic garment simulation techniques, and the application of diffusion models to other human motion-related tasks. The training strategy could serve as a template for other tasks where real-world data is scarce or noisy. The paper could be particularly useful to researchers studying human motion in unconstrained settings.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper addresses an important, practical problem and presents a novel solution using state-of-the-art techniques.  The results demonstrate a clear improvement over existing methods. While the core diffusion model architecture isn't groundbreaking, the application to loose-IMU pose estimation, the clever data synthesis strategy, and the garment parameter integration make a significant contribution. While TailorNet is an assumption, and more detailed work on how the different garment parameters effect estimation in real-world is a need for the future, the contributions are valuable to the community.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 7/10

### MEGC2025: Micro-Expression Grand Challenge on Spot Then Recognize and Visual Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15298v1)
- **Authors**: Xinqi Fan, Jingting Li, John See, Moi Hoon Yap, Wen-Huang Cheng, Xiaobai Li, Xiaopeng Hong, Su-Jing Wang, Adrian K. Davision
